## V Melnikoff / 五 梅利尼科夫
===Original===

Some three weeks later, on a cold Sunday morning in January, I sat in the Doctor’s study at his small flat in one of the big houses at the end of the Kamenostrovsky Prospect. The news had just arrived that the German Communist leaders, Karl Liebknecht and Rosa Luxembourg, had been killed in Berlin, the former in attempted flight, the latter mobbed by an incensed crowd. Nobody in Russia had any idea who these two people were, but their deaths caused consternation in the Communist camp, for they had been relied upon to pull off a Red revolution in Germany and thus accelerate the wave of Bolshevism westward across Europe.
Little known as Liebknecht and Luxembourg had been outside Germany until the time of their death, in the hierarchy of Bolshevist saints they were placed second only to Karl Marx and Engels, the Moses and Aaron of the Communist Party. Russians are noted for their veneration of icons, representing to them the memory of saintly lives, but their religious devotion is equalled by that of the Bolsheviks. Though he does not cross himself, the true Bolshevik bows down in spirit to the images of Marx and kindred revolutionaries with an obsequiousness unexcelled by devotees of the church. The difference in the two creeds lies in this: that whereas the orthodox Christian venerates saintly lives according to their degree of unworldliness, individual goodness, and spiritual sanctity, the Bolsheviks revere their saints for the vehemence with which they promoted the class war, fomented discontent, and preached worldwide revolution.
To what extent humanity suffered as the result of the decease of the two German Communists, I am unable to judge, but their loss was regarded by the revolutionary leaders as a catastrophe of the first magnitude. The official Press had heavy headlines about it, and those who read the papers asked one another who the two individuals could have been. Having studied the revolutionary movement to some extent, I was better able to appreciate the mortification of the ruling party, and was therefore interested in the great public demonstration announced for that day in honour of the dead.
My new friend, the Doctor, was both puzzled and amused by my attitude.
“I can understand your being here as an intelligence officer,” he said. “After all, your Government has to have someone to keep them informed, though it must be unpleasant for you.

===Chinese===

大约三个星期后，一月里一个寒冷的星期日早晨，我坐在医生的书房里——他的小公寓在卡缅诺斯特罗夫大街尽头一幢大宅里。消息刚刚传来：德国共产党领袖卡尔·李卜克内西和罗莎·卢森堡在柏林遇害，前者死于逃跑未遂，后者被激愤的人群围殴致死。俄国没有人知道这两位是谁，可他们的死却在共产党营垒中引起一片惊惶，因为大家原指望他们能在德国搞成一场红色革命，从而把布尔什维主义的浪潮加速推向欧洲西部。

李卜克内西和卢森堡直到死前，在德国以外知之者甚少；然而在布尔什维克的圣徒谱系里，他们被安置在仅次于卡尔·马克思和恩格斯的位置——那两位是共产党的摩西与亚伦。俄国人以崇奉圣像著称，圣像对他们意味着圣洁生涯的记忆；而他们的宗教虔诚，正可以同布尔什维克相比肩。真正的布尔什维克虽不画十字，却在精神上向马克思及诸革命家的圣像俯首下拜，那份卑躬屈膝，连教会的虔徒也望尘莫及。两种信仰的分野在于：正统基督徒之崇奉圣徒，是按其超脱尘世、个人德行与灵性圣洁的程度；布尔什维克之敬仰他们的圣徒，却在于其鼓动阶级战争、煽布不满、宣讲世界革命的激烈程度。

这两位德国共产党人之死使人类蒙受了多大损失，我无从评判，但革命领袖们把他们的死视为头等的灾难。官方报纸为之登出通栏大标题，读报的人互相打听这两位究竟是什么人。我对革命运动多少有些研究，较能体会执政党的这番痛楚，因此对当日宣布举行的悼念死者的大规模群众示威，颇感兴趣。

我的新朋友医生，对我的态度又是困惑又觉好笑。

"你作为情报军官待在这儿，这个我能理解，"他说，"毕竟你们政府总得有人给它通消息，虽说这差事对你想必并不好受。

===Original===

But why you should take it into your head to go rushing round to all the silly meetings and demonstrations the way you do is beyond me. And the stuff you read! You have only been here three or four times, but you have left a train of papers and pamphlets enough to open a propaganda department.”
The Doctor, who I learned from the woman at the lodge was Melnikoff’s uncle, was a splendid fellow. As a matter of fact, he had sided wholeheartedly with the revolution in March, 1917, and held very radical views, but he thought more than spoke about them. His nephew, Melnikoff, on the contrary, together with a considerable group of officers, had opposed the revolution from the outset, but the Doctor had not quarrelled with them, realizing one cardinal truth the Bolsheviks appear to fail to grasp, namely, that the criterion whereby men must ultimately be judged is not politics, but character.
The Doctor had a young and very intelligent friend named Shura, who had been a bosom friend of Melnikoff’s. Shura was a law student. He resembled the Doctor in his radical sympathies but differed from both him and Melnikoff in that he was given to philosophizing and probing deeply beneath the surface of things. Many were the discussions we had together, when, some weeks later, I came to know Shura well.
“Communist speeches,” he used to say, “often sound like a tale told by an idiot, full of sound and fury signifying nothing. But behind the interminable jargon there lie both an impulse and an ideal. The ideal is a proletarian millennium, but the impulse is not love of the worker, but hatred of the bourgeois. The Bolshevik believes that if a perfect proletarian state be forcibly established by destroying the bourgeoisie, the perfect proletarian citizen will automatically result! There will be no crime, no prisons, no need of government. But by persecuting liberals and denying freedom of thought the Bolsheviks are driving independent thinkers into the camp of that very section of society whose provocative conduct caused Bolshevism! That is why I will fight to oust the Bolsheviks,” said Shura, “they are impediments in the path of the revolution.”
It had been a strange interview when I first called on the Doctor and announced myself as a friend of Melnikoff’s. He sat bolt upright, smiling affably, and obviously ready for every conceivable contingency.

===Chinese===

可你干吗突发奇想，成天往那些愚蠢的集会和示威里凑，我实在闹不懂。还有你读的那些玩意儿！你统共才来过三四回，落下的报纸和小册子，足够开一个宣传部了。"

医生——我后来从门房女人那里得知，他是梅利尼科夫的舅舅——是个极好的人。事实上，1917年三月革命时他是全心全意站在革命一边的，见解也十分激进；只是他想得多，说得少。他外甥梅利尼科夫则相反，同一批相当的军官一道，从一开头就反对革命；医生却同他们没有反目，因为他悟得一条布尔什维克们似乎始终参不透的根本道理：衡量一个人的最终标准，不是政治，而是品性。

医生有一位年轻而极聪明的朋友，名叫舒拉，原是梅利尼科夫的莫逆之交。舒拉是个法科学生。论激进的同情，他同医生一路；但他别于医生和梅利尼科夫之处，在于喜好哲思，惯于往事情的表层底下深挖。几个星期后我同舒拉相熟起来，我们在一起有过许多次长谈。

"共产党人的演说，"他常说，"往往听着像痴人说梦，充满了声音与喧嚣，却毫无意义。可在那套没完没了的行话背后，既有一种冲动，也有一种理想。理想是无产阶级的千年王国，冲动却不是对工人的爱，而是对资产者的恨。布尔什维克相信：只要用强力消灭资产阶级、建成完美的无产阶级国家，完美的无产阶级公民便会自动产生！到那时没有犯罪，没有监狱，也无须政府。可是，布尔什维克一面迫害自由派、否定思想自由，一面正把独立思考的人们赶进社会中那个阶层的营垒——当初正是那个阶层的挑衅行径催生了布尔什维主义！所以我才要斗争，把布尔什维克赶下台，"舒拉说，"他们是革命道路上的绊脚石。"

我头一回去拜访医生、自称是梅利尼科夫的朋友时，那是一场古怪的会面。他坐得笔直，和蔼地微笑着，显然对一切可能的意外都有备无患。

===Original===

The last thing in the world he was prepared to do was to believe me. I told him all I could about his nephew and he evidently thought I was very clever to know so much. He was polite but categorical. No, sir, he knew nothing whatsoever of his nephew’s movements, it was good of me to interest myself in his welfare, but he himself had ceased to be interested. I might possibly be an Englishman, as I said, but he had never heard his nephew mention an Englishman. He had no knowledge nor any desire for information as to his nephew’s past, present, or future, and if his nephew had engaged in counterrevolutionary activities it was his own fault. I could not but admire the placidity and suavity with which he said all this, and cursed the disguise which made me look so unlike what I wanted the Doctor to see.
“Do you speak English?” I said at last, getting exasperated.
I detected a twinge﻿—ever so slight. “A little,” he replied.
“Then, damn it all, man,” I exclaimed in English, rising and striking my chest with my fist﻿—rather melodramatically, it must have seemed﻿—“why the devil can’t you see I *am* an Englishman and not a provocateur? Melnikoff must have told you something about me. Except for me he wouldn’t have come back here. Didn’t he tell you how we stayed together at Viborg, how he helped dress me, how he drank all my whisky, how﻿—?”
The Doctor all at once half rose from his seat. The urbane, fixed smile that had not left his lips since the beginning of the interview suddenly burst into a half-laugh.
“Was it *you* who gave him the whisky?” he broke in, in Russian.
“Of course it was,” I replied. “I﻿—”
“That settles it,” he said, excitedly. “Sit down; I’ll be back in a moment.”
He left the room and walked quickly to the front door. Half suspecting treachery, I peered out into the hall and feeling for the small revolver I carried, looked round to see if there were any way of escape in an emergency. The Doctor opened the front door, stepped on to the landing, looked carefully up and down the stairs, and, returning, closed all the other doors in the hall before reentering the study. He walked over to where I stood and looked me straight in the face.
“Why on earth didn’t you come before?” he exclaimed, speaking in a low voice.
---
We rapidly became friends. Melnikoff’s disappearance had been a complete mystery to him, a mystery which he had no means of solving.

===Chinese===

他最不打算做的事，就是相信我。我把关于他外甥的事尽我所知说了，他显然觉得我知道得这么多，实在高明。他客气，却斩钉截铁。不，先生，外甥的行踪他一概不知；我肯关心他外甥的安危，承情之至，但他自己早已不再关心了。我或许真是英国人，如我所说；可他从未听外甥提起过什么英国人。外甥的过去、现在与将来，他一无所知，也无心打听；倘若外甥参加了反革命活动，那是他自作自受。他说这些话时那份镇定与圆融，我不能不佩服；同时暗暗咒骂我这一身化装，害我看上去同我希望医生认出的那个人判若两人。

"您会讲英语吗？"我终于按捺不住，问道。

我捕捉到一丝抽动——轻微得很。"会一点。"他答。

"那么，见鬼，老兄！"我改用英语嚷起来，站起身，一拳捶在自己胸口——想必显得相当做作——"你他妈为什么看不出我*就是*个英国人，不是密探？梅利尼科夫总该同你讲过我。没有我，他才不会回这儿来。他没告诉过你我们俩一起待在维堡的事？没说他怎么帮我穿衣裳、怎么喝光了我的威士忌、怎么——？"

医生猛地半从椅子上站起。自会面开始便挂在他唇边的那种彬彬有礼、纹丝不动的微笑，霎时绽成了半个大笑。

"给过他威士忌的，就是*你*？"他用俄语插进来。

"当然是我，"我答，"我——"

"这就对了，"他激动地说，"坐好；我去去就来。"

他出了房间，快步走向前门。我半疑有诈，探头向门厅张望，一面摸着随身带的小左轮，一面环顾四周，看紧急时有没有脱身之路。只见医生打开前门，走到楼梯口，上上下下仔细望了一遭，回身把门厅里其余的门一一关严，才重新回到书房。他走到我站立的地方，直直望着我的脸。

"你到底为什么不早来？"他压低嗓子嚷道。

---

我们很快成了朋友。梅利尼科夫的失踪，对他始终是个彻底的谜，一个他无从破解的谜。

===Original===

He had never heard of Zorinsky, but names meant nothing. He thought it strange that so high a price should be demanded for Melnikoff, and thought I had been unwise to give it all in advance under any circumstances; but he was none the less overjoyed to hear of the prospects of his release.
After every visit to Zorinsky I called on the Doctor to tell him the latest news. On this particular morning I had told him how the evening before, in a manner which I disliked intensely, Zorinsky had shelved the subject, giving evasive answers. We had passed the middle of January already, yet apparently there was no information whatever as to Melnikoff’s case.
“There is another thing, too, that disquiets me, Doctor,” I added. “Zorinsky shows undue curiosity as to where I go when I am not at his house. He happens to know the passport on which I am living, and examination of papers being so frequent, I wish I could get another one. Have you any idea what Melnikoff would do in such circumstances?”
The Doctor paced up and down the room.
“Would you mind telling me the name?” he asked.
I showed him all my documents, including the exemption certificate, explaining how I had received them.
“Well, well, your Mr. Zorinsky certainly is a useful friend to have, I must say,” he observed, looking at the certificate, and wagging his head knowingly. “By the way, does he cost you much, if one may ask?”
“He himself? Nothing at all, or very little. Besides the sixty thousand for Melnikoff,” I calculated, “I have given him a few thousand for odd expenses connected with the case; I insist on paying for meals; I gave his wife an expensive bouquet at New Year with which she was very pleased; then I have given him money for the relief of Melnikoff’s sister, and﻿—”
“For Melnikoff’s sister?” ejaculated the Doctor. “But he hasn’t got one!”
*Vot tibie ná!* No sister﻿—then where did the money go? I suddenly remembered Zorinsky had once asked if I could give him English money. I told the Doctor.
“Look out, my friend, look out,” he said. “Your friend is certainly a clever and a useful man. But I’m afraid you will have to go on paying for Melnikoff’s nonexistent sister. It would not do for him to know you had found out. As for your passport, I will ask Shura. By the way,” he added, “it is twelve o’clock. Will you not be late for your precious demonstration?”
I hurried to leave.

===Chinese===

他从未听说过佐林斯基，不过姓名说明不了什么。他觉得为梅利尼科夫索这么高的价钱很蹊跷，也认为我无论如何不该把钱全部预付——不智之至；但听我说起有获释的希望，仍旧喜出望外。

每次从佐林斯基那里回来，我都去医生家报告最新消息。这个特别的早晨，我已经告诉他：前一晚，佐林斯基又用我极为反感的那套手法把话题岔开，答非所问。一月已过了一半，梅利尼科夫的案子却显然毫无音讯。

"还有一件事也让我不安，医生，"我补充道，"我不在他家的时候去哪儿，佐林斯基表现出过分的好奇。他恰好知道我眼下赖以为生的那张护照；而查验证件又这样频繁，我真想再弄一张。您猜梅利尼科夫处在这种境地会怎么办？"

医生在屋里踱来踱去。

"能不能把名字告诉我？"他问。

我把全部证件拿给他看，包括那张免役证，并说明这些证件的来历。

"啧啧，你那位佐林斯基先生可真是个有用的朋友，我得说，"他端详着免役证，煞有介事地点着头，"顺便问一句，要是可以问的话——他花掉你不少钱吧？"

"他本人？一点也不，或者说微不足道。除了为梅利尼科夫付的六万，"我盘算着，"我还为案子里的零星开销给过他几千；饭钱我一概坚持自付；新年我送了他太太一束贵重的花，她高兴得很；另外我还给过他一笔钱，接济梅利尼科夫的妹妹，还有——"

"梅利尼科夫的妹妹？"医生失声道，"可他根本没有妹妹！"

*Vot tibie ná!*（好家伙！）没有妹妹——那钱进了谁的腰包？我猛然想起，佐林斯基有一次问过我能不能给他英镑。我把这事告诉了医生。

"当心，我的朋友，当心，"他说，"你这位朋友固然又精明又有用。可我看，这位梅利尼科夫'并不存在的妹妹'，你还得继续养下去。叫他知道你识破了，那可不妙。至于护照，我去问问舒拉。对了，"他补了一句，"十二点了。你那宝贵的示威，不怕迟到吗？"

我匆忙告辞。

===Original===

“I will let you know how things go,” I said. “I will be back in two or three days.”
The morning was a frosty one with a bitter wind. No streetcars ran on Sundays and I walked into town to the Palace Square, the great space in front of the Winter Palace, famous for another January Sunday﻿—“Bloody Sunday”﻿—thirteen years before. Much had been made in the Press of the present occasion, and it appeared to be taken for granted that the proletariat would surge to bear testimony to their grief for the fallen German Communists. But round the base of a red-bedizened tribune in the centre of the square there clustered a mere handful of people and two rows of soldiers, stamping to keep their feet warm. The crowd consisted of the sturdy Communist veterans who organized the demonstration and onlookers who always join any throng to see whatever is going on.
As usual the proceedings started late, and the small but patient crowd was beginning to dwindle before the chief speakers arrived. A group of commonplace-looking individuals, standing on the tribune, lounged and smoked cigarettes, apparently not knowing exactly what to do with themselves. I pushed myself forward to be as near the speakers as possible.
To my surprise I noticed Dmitri, Stepanovna’s nephew, among the soldiers who stood blowing on their hands and looking miserable. I moved a few steps away, so that he might not see me. I was afraid he would make some sign of recognition which might lead to questions by his comrades, and I had no idea who they might be. But I was greatly amused at seeing him at a demonstration of this sort.
At length an automobile dashed up, and amid faint cheers and to the accompaniment of bugles, Zinoviev, president of the Petrograd Soviet, alighted and mounted the tribune. Zinoviev, whose real name is Apfelbaum, is a very important person in Bolshevist Russia. He is considered one of the greatest orators of the Communist Party, and now occupies the proud position of president of the Third International, the institution that is to effect the world revolution.
It is to his oratorical skill rather than any administrative ability that Zinoviev owes his prominence. His rhetoric is of a peculiar order.

===Chinese===

"我会把情况告诉您的，"我说，"两三天后回来。"

那天早晨天寒地冻，风刮得刺骨。星期日无电车可乘，我便步行进城，走到宫殿广场——冬宫前的大广场，十三年前另一个一月的星期日使它名闻天下，那就是"流血星期日"。为眼前这次集会，报纸已大肆渲染了一番，似乎想当然地以为无产阶级会潮涌而至，来为殒命的德国共产党人作证他们的哀思。可是，广场中央那座披红挂彩的检阅台底下，拢共只聚着一小撮人和两列士兵，跺着脚取暖。人堆里，一部分是组织这场示威的坚定的共产党老积极分子，一部分是那种逢热闹必凑的看客。

仪式照例迟迟不开场，等主要演讲人到场时，那一小群耐心的听众已开始散去。台上站着几个相貌平常的人物，懒洋洋地抽着烟，显然不知道自己该干什么好。我往前挤，尽量靠近演讲人。

让我吃惊的是，我在士兵堆里看见了德米特里——斯捷潘诺夫娜的侄子；他正朝手上呵气，一脸苦相。我挪开几步，免得被他瞧见。我怕他认出我会做出什么表示，引来他战友们的盘问——他们是什么人，我可心里没底。不过在这样一种示威上见到他，我不禁大为有趣。

终于，一辆汽车疾驰而至；在稀稀落落的欢呼声和军号声中，彼得格勒苏维埃主席季诺维也夫下车登上检阅台。季诺维也夫，真名阿普费尔鲍姆，是布尔什维克俄国的一位要人。他被认为是共产党最伟大的演说家之一，如今又身居第三国际主席这一显赫的位置——第三国际，正是那个将要完成世界革命的机构。

季诺维也夫的显赫，靠的是演说的本事，而非任何行政才干。他的雄辩自成一格。

===Original===

He is unrivalled in his appeal to the ignorant mob, but, judging by his speeches, logic is unknown to him, and on no thinking audience could he produce any impression beyond that of wonderment at his uncommon command of language, ready though cheap witticisms, and inexhaustible fund of florid and vulgar invective. Zinoviev is, in fact, the consummate gutter-demagogue. He is a coward, shirked office in November, 1917, fearing the instability of the Bolshevist coup, has since been chief advocate of all the insaner aspects of Bolshevism, and is always the first to lose his head and fly into a panic when danger-clouds appear on any horizon.
Removing his hat, Zinoviev approached the rail, and stood there in his rich fur coat until someone down below gave a signal to cheer. Then he began to speak in the following strain:
“Comrades! Wherefore are we gathered here today? What mean this tribune and this concourse of people? Is it to celebrate a triumph of world revolution, to hail another conquest over the vicious ogre of Capitalism? Alas, no! Today we mourn the two greatest heroes of our age, murdered deliberately, brutally, and in cold blood by blackguard capitalist agents. The German Government, consisting of the social-traitor Scheidemann and other supposed Socialists, the scum and dregs of humanity, have sold themselves like Judas Iscariot for thirty shekels of silver to the German bourgeoisie, and at the command of the capitalists ordered their paid hirelings foully to murder the two chosen representatives of the German workers and peasants﻿ ﻿…” and so on.
I never listened to Zinoviev without recalling a meeting in the summer of 1917 when he was the chief speaker. He had just returned to Russia with a group of other Bolshevist leaders (very few of whom were present during the revolution) and was holding incendiary meetings in out-of-the-way places. He was thin and slim and looked the typical Jewish student of any Russian university. But after a year’s fattening on the Russian proletariat he had swelled not only politically but physically, and his full, handsome features and flowing bushy hair spoke of anything but privation.
Contrary to custom, Zinoviev’s speech was short. It must have been cold, speaking in the chilly wind, and in any case there were not many people to talk to.

===Chinese===

他蛊惑无知群氓的本事无与伦比；可从他的演说来看，逻辑于他乃是闻所未闻之物。在任何会思考的听众面前，他所能引起的，无非是对他那不同寻常的驱遣语言之才、张口即来却俗不可耐的俏皮话、以及取之不竭的华丽而粗鄙的谩骂的惊叹。季诺维也夫实际上是个登峰造极的街头煽动家。他还是个懦夫：1917年11月，因惧怕布尔什维克政变根基不稳，他畏而避职；此后却成了布尔什维主义一切更疯狂方面的首席鼓吹者；而每逢任何地平线上阴云乍起，他总是头一个失魂落魄、惊慌失措。

季诺维也夫摘下帽子，走近栏杆，穿着华贵的皮大衣站在那里，直到台下有人发出欢呼的信号。然后他开口了，调门如下：

"同志们！我们今日为何在此集会？这座检阅台、这人群，意味着什么？是为了庆祝世界革命的胜利，欢呼对资本主义这个恶妖的又一次征服吗？唉，不是！今天，我们哀悼我们时代最伟大的两位英雄——他们被资本主义的恶棍走狗蓄意地、残忍地、冷血地谋杀了。由社会叛徒谢德曼和其他所谓的社会主义者——人类的人渣败类——组成的德国政府，像加略人犹大一样，为三十舍客勒银子把自己出卖给了德国资产阶级，并遵照资本家的命令，指使其雇佣的走狗卑劣地谋害了德国工农这两位选出的代表……"如此等等。

每听季诺维也夫演说，我总会想起1917年夏天的一次集会，那次他是主讲人。他刚同其他一批布尔什维克领袖（革命发生时他们在场的寥寥无几）回到俄国，正在偏僻处开煽风点火的会。那时他瘦削细长，一副俄国大学里典型的犹太学生模样。可是在俄国无产阶级身上贴膘一年之后，他不但在政治上、也在体格上发了起来：那副丰满英俊的面孔和浓密蓬松的长发，说的哪里是半点匮乏。

一反惯例，季诺维也夫这次讲得很短。迎着寒风讲话想必够冷的了，何况本来也没有多少人可讲。

===Original===

The next speaker was more novel﻿—Herr Otto Pertz, president of the German Soviet of Petrograd. Why a German Soviet continued to live and move and have its being in Petrograd, or what its functions were, nobody seemed to know. The comings and goings of *unsere deutsche Genossen* appeared to be above criticism and were always a mystery. Herr Otto Pertz was tall, clean shaven, Germanly tidy, and could not speak Russian.
“*Genossen! heute feiern wir*﻿—” he began, and proceeded to laud the memory of the fallen heroes and to foretell the coming social revolution in Germany. The dastardly tyrants of Berlin, insolently styling themselves Socialists, would shortly be overthrown. *Kapitalismus*, *Imperialismus*, in fact everything but *Kommunismus*, would be demolished. He had information that within a week or two Spartacus (the German Bolshevist group), with all Germany behind it, would successfully seize power in Berlin and join in a triumphant and indissoluble alliance with the Russian Socialist Federative Soviet Republic.
As Otto Pertz commenced his oration a neatly dressed little lady of about fifty, who stood at my side near the foot of the tribune, looked up eagerly at the speaker. Her eyes shone brightly and her breath came quickly. Seeing I had noticed her she said timidly, “*Spricht er nicht gut? Sagen Sie doch, spricht er nicht gut?*”
To which I of course replied, “*Sehr gut*,” and she relapsed bashfully into admiration of Otto, murmuring now and again, “*Ach! es ist doch wahr, nicht?*” with which sentiment also I would agree.
The crowd listened patiently, as the Russian crowd always listens, whoever speaks, and on whatever subject. The soldiers shivered and wondered what the speaker was talking about. His speech was not translated.
But when Otto Pertz ceased there was a commotion in the throng. For some moments I was at a loss as to what was in progress, until at last a passage was made and, borne on valiant Communist shoulders, a guy, the special attraction of the day, was produced. The effigy, made of pasteboard, represented a ferocious-looking German with Kaiser-like moustachios, clothed in evening dress, and bearing across its chest in large letters on cardboard the name of the German Socialist,

> Scheidemann.

===Chinese===

下一位演讲人更新奇——奥托·佩尔茨先生，彼得格勒德国苏维埃主席。为什么一个德国苏维埃竟在彼得格勒生存活动，它的职能又是什么，似乎谁也不知道。*我们德国同志*（*unsere deutsche Genossen*）的来来去去俨然不容置评，始终是个谜。奥托·佩尔茨先生高个子，脸刮得干干净净，一副德国式的整洁，不会讲俄语。

"*Genossen! heute feiern wir——*（同志们！今天我们庆祝——）"他这样起头，接着颂扬殒命英雄们的英灵，预言德国社会革命之将临。柏林那些无耻的暴君，竟厚着脸皮自称社会主义者，不久就要被推翻。*Kapitalismus*（资本主义）、*Imperialismus*（帝国主义）——总之除*Kommunismus*（共产主义）以外的一切——都将被摧毁。他掌握情报：一两周之内，斯巴达克团（德国的布尔什维克团体）将在全德国的拥护下，成功夺取柏林政权，同俄罗斯社会主义联邦苏维埃共和国结成凯旋的、牢不可破的同盟。

奥托·佩尔茨开讲时，我身旁检阅台脚下站着一位衣着整洁的小个子太太，五十上下年纪，热切地仰望着演讲人。她眼睛发亮，呼吸急促。见我注意到了她，她怯生生地问："*Spricht er nicht gut? Sagen Sie doch, spricht er nicht gut?*（他讲得不好吗？您说说，他讲得不好吗？）"

我当然答以"*Sehr gut*（好极了）"，她便又害羞地沉浸在对奥托的倾慕里，不时喃喃着"*Ach! es ist doch wahr, nicht?*（啊！这可是真的，不是吗？）"——这一层高见，我也照例同意。

人群耐心听着——俄国的群众向来如此，无论谁讲，也无论讲什么。士兵们打着哆嗦，纳闷演讲人讲的是什么名堂。他的演讲没人翻译。

奥托·佩尔茨一住口，人群中便起了骚动。起初我好一阵摸不着头脑，不知正演着哪一出，直到人群让开一条道，只见一具草人——当日的特别看点——被英勇的共产党员们扛在肩上抬了出来。那纸板扎的假人，一副凶神恶煞的德国人模样，蓄着德皇式的小胡子，穿着晚礼服，胸口横着一块纸板，上书那位德国社会党人的大名：

> 谢德曼。

===Original===

At the same time an improvised gallows was thrust over the balustrade of the tribune. Amid curses, jeers, and execrations, the moustachioed effigy was raised aloft. Eager hands attached the dangling loop and there it hung, most abject, most melancholy, encased in evening dress, and black trousers with hollow extremities flapping in the breeze.
The crowd awoke and tittered and even the soldiers smiled. Dmitri, I could see, was laughing outright. This was after all worth coming to see. Kerosene was poured on the dangling Scheidemann and he was set alight. There were laughter, howls, and fanfares. Zinoviev, in tragic pose, with uplifted arm and pointed finger, cried hoarsely, “Thus perish traitors!” The bugles blew. The people, roused with delight, cheered lustily. Only the wretched Scheidemann was indifferent to the interest he was arousing, as with a stony glare on his cardboard face he soared aloft amid sparks and ashes into eternity.
Crowd psychology, I mused as I walked away, has been an important factor on all public occasions since the revolution, but appreciated to the full only by the Bolsheviks. Everyone who was in Russia in 1917 and who attended political meetings when free speech became a possibility remembers how a speaker would get up and speak, loudly applauded by the whole audience; then another would rise and say the precise opposite, rewarded with equally vociferous approbation; followed again by a third who said something totally at variance with the first two, and how the enthusiasm would increase in proportion to the uncertainty as to who was actually right. The crowds were just like little children. Totally unaccustomed to free speech, they appeared to imagine that anybody who spoke must ipso facto be right. But just when the people, after the Bolshevist coup d’état, were beginning to demand reason in public utterance and deeds instead of promises, down came a super-Tsarist Bolshevist censorship like a huge candle-snuffer and clapping itself on the flame of public criticism, snuffed it out altogether.
Public demonstrations, however, were made an important item in the curriculum of the Bolshevist administration, and soon became as compulsory as military service.

===Chinese===

与此同时，一座临时搭起的绞架从检阅台栏杆上探了出去。咒骂声、哄笑声、叫骂声里，那具蓄着小胡子的假人被高高吊起。急切的手把晃荡的绳圈系好，它便挂在了那里，无比狼狈，无比凄凉，裹着晚礼服，黑裤子的空裤管在风中扑扑地摆。

人群醒过来了，窃笑着，连士兵们也笑了。我看得出，德米特里正放声大笑。这到底不枉来看一场。煤油浇上悬着的谢德曼，点着了火。笑声、嚎叫、号声齐作。季诺维也夫摆出悲壮的姿势，手臂高举，手指前指，嘶声喊道："叛徒就当这样灭亡！"号角齐鸣。群众兴高采烈，纵情欢呼。唯有那倒霉的谢德曼，对激起的热闹无动于衷，纸板脸上凝着一副石头发怔的表情，在火星与灰烬中冉冉升空，飞进了永恒。

我一面走开一面寻思：自革命以来，群众心理在一切公众场合都是个重要因素，而唯有布尔什维克把它用到了十足。1917年身在俄国、赶上言论自由初露曙光时出席过政治集会的人，都记得那番景象：一个演讲人站起来高谈阔论，全场掌声雷动；接着另一个起身，说的恰恰相反，同样博得震天的喝彩；随后第三个人再起来，说的与前两位全然相左，而随着"到底谁对"的越发糊涂，热情反倒越发高涨。群众就跟小孩子一模一样。他们全然不惯于言论自由，仿佛认定：凡是登台开口的人，必然就是对的一方。可正当人民在布尔什维克政变之后，开始在公开的言论和行为中要求理性而非空诺时，一顶超级沙皇式的布尔什维克书报检查制度压了下来，像一只巨大的烛罩，扣在公众批评的火苗上，把它整个儿掐灭了。

不过，群众示威被立为布尔什维克行政课目中的重要一项，不久便同兵役一样成了义务。

===Original===

I record the above one not because of its intrinsic interest (it really had very little), but because it was, I believe, one of the last occasions on which it was left to the public to make the demonstration a success or not, and regiments were merely “invited.”
I made my way to Stepanovna’s in the hope of meeting Dmitri. He came in toward the close of the afternoon, and I asked him if he had enjoyed the demonstration.
“Too cold,” he replied; “they ought to have had it on a warmer day.”
“Did you come voluntarily?”
“Why, yes.” He pulled out of the spacious pocket of his tunic a parcel wrapped up in newspaper, and unwrapping it, disclosed a pound of bread. “We were told we should get this if we came. It has just been doled out.”
Stepanovna’s eyes opened wide. Deeply interested, she asked when the next demonstration was going to be.
“Why didn’t more soldiers come, then?” I asked.
“Not enough bread, I suppose,” said Dmitri. “We have been getting it irregularly of late. But we have a new commissar who is a good fellow. They say in the regiment he gets everything for us first. He talks to us decently, too. I am beginning to like him. Perhaps he is not one like the rest.”
“By the way, Dmitri,” I said, “do you happen to know who those people were for whom we demonstrated today?”
From the depths of his crumb-filled pocket Dmitri extracted a crumpled and soiled pamphlet. Holding it to the light he slowly read out the title: “Who Were Karl Liebknecht and Rosa Luxembourg?”
“We were each given one yesterday,” he explained, “after an agitator had made a long speech to us. Nobody listened to the agitator﻿—some Jew or other﻿—but the commissar gave me this. I read little nowadays, but I think I will read it when I have time.”
“And the speakers and the guy?” I queried.
“I didn’t notice the speakers. One of them spoke not in our way﻿—German, someone said. But the guy! That was funny! My, Stepanovna, you ought to have seen it! How it floated up into the air! You would have split your sides laughing. Who was it supposed to represent, by the way?”
I explained how the revolution in Germany had resulted in the downfall of the Kaiser and the formation of a radical Cabinet with a Socialist﻿—Scheidemann﻿—at its head. Scheidemann was the guy today, I said, for reasons which I presumed he would find stated in *Who Were Karl Liebknecht and Rosa Luxembourg?

===Chinese===

我记下这一场，并非因为它本身有多少趣味（实在寥寥），而是因为我相信，这是最后几回还任凭公众决定示威成不成场面、而各团只是"受邀"参加的一次。

我往斯捷潘诺夫娜家去，指望碰上德米特里。临近傍晚时分他回来了，我问他示威看得可好。

"太冷了，"他答，"该挑个暖和的日子搞。"

"你们是自愿去的？"

"那当然。"他从军服宽敞的口袋里掏出一个报纸包，打开来，露出一磅面包。"他们说了，来了就发这个。刚发的。"

斯捷潘诺夫娜眼睛睁得溜圆，大感兴趣，问下一回示威定在哪天。

"那怎么没多来些士兵？"我问。

"面包不够呗，我猜，"德米特里说，"近来发得时断时续。不过我们新来了位政委，是个好人。团里都说，什么东西他都先给我们弄来。他跟我们说话也像个人样。我有点喜欢他了。兴许他跟别的不一路。"

"对了，德米特里，"我说，"今天咱们示威悼念的那两位，你知道是什么人吗？"

德米特里从那个满是面包渣的口袋深处，摸出一本皱巴巴的脏小册子，举到亮处，慢慢念出书名：《卡尔·李卜克内西和罗莎·卢森堡是何许人？》

"一人发了一本，昨天发的，"他解释说，"先是一个鼓动员对我们作了长篇训话。谁也没听那鼓动员的——一个什么犹太人——倒是政委给了我这本。如今我很少看书，不过得空了倒想读读。"

"那些演讲的和那个草人呢？"我问。

"演讲的没留意。有一个讲的不是咱们的话——有人说是德语。可那草人！真逗！哎呀，斯捷潘诺夫娜，你真该看看！它怎么一下子飞上天去！准能叫你笑破肚皮。对了，它装的是谁来着？"

我便解释：德国革命的结果，是德皇垮台，组成了一个由社会党人谢德曼为首的激进内阁。今天烧的草人就是谢德曼，我说；至于为什么，我料想他能在《卡尔·李卜克内西和罗莎·卢森堡是何许人？》里读到。

===Original===

*
“But if the Kaiser is out, why do our Bolsheviks burn﻿—what’s his name﻿—?”
“Ah, but, Dmitri,” I put in, “if you had understood the German speaker today, you would have heard him tell how there is shortly to be another revolution in Germany like that which happened here in November, 1917, and they will set up a Soviet Government like Lenin’s.”
As our conversation proceeded, Stepanovna and Varia stopped their work to listen, their interest grew apace, and at last they hung on to every word as if it were of profound significance. When I repeated the substance of Otto Pertz’s predictions, all three of my companions were listening spellbound and with mouths agape. There was a long pause, which at length Stepanovna broke.
“Is it really possible,” she exclaimed, slowly, and apparently in utter bewilderment, “that the Germans﻿—are﻿—such﻿—fools?”
---
“Evasive, Doctor, very evasive,” I said, as we sat over tea and a few dry biscuits the Doctor had procured from somewhere. “Yesterday evening he gave me some interesting information about industrial developments, alteration of railway administration, and changes in the Red fleet; but the moment Melnikoff is mentioned then it is, ‘Oh, Melnikoff? in a day or two I think we may know definitely,’ or ‘My informant is out of town,’ and so on.”
“Perhaps there is a hitch somewhere,” suggested the Doctor. “I suppose there is nothing to do but wait. By the way, you wanted a passport, didn’t you? How will that suit you?”
I have forgotten the precise wording of the paper he handed me, for I had to destroy it later, but it was an ordinary certificate of identification, in the name of Alexander Vasilievitch Markovitch, aged thirty-three, clerical assistant at the head Postal-Telegraph Office. There was no photograph attached, but in view of the strict requirements regarding passports, which included their frequent renewal (except in certain cases no passports might be made out for more than two months), and the difficulty of getting photographs, the latter were dropping out of general use.
“Shura procured it,” the Doctor explained. “A friend of his, by name Markov, arrived recently from Moscow to work at the Telegraph Office. A week later he heard his wife was seriously ill and got special permission to return. A week in Petrograd was enough for him anyway, for living is much better in Moscow, so he doesn’t intend to come back.

===Chinese===

"可德皇既然下了台，咱们布尔什维克干吗还烧那个——他叫什么来着——？"

"啊，可是，德米特里，"我插话道，"今天那位德国演讲人的话你若是听得懂，就会听见他讲：德国不久还要来一场革命，跟咱们这儿1917年11月那场一样，他们也要建立一个同列宁的一样的苏维埃政府。"

我们这样谈着，斯捷潘诺夫娜和瓦丽娅掂下手里的活计凑过来听，兴味越来越浓，到后来一字不落地听着，仿佛字字都有千钧之重。待我复述完奥托·佩尔茨的预言，三位同伴听得入了定，嘴都张着。半晌无言，最后是斯捷潘诺夫娜开的口。

"难道说，"她慢吞吞地嚷道，显然全然懵了，"德国人——真——是——这样的——傻瓜？"

---

"支吾，医生，尽是支吾，"我说。我们正对坐喝茶，就着几块不知他从哪儿弄来的干饼干。"昨天晚上，他还给了我一些有意思的消息，讲工业发展、铁路管理改制、红海军的变动；可一提梅利尼科夫，立刻就是'哦，梅利尼科夫？我想一两天内就能有个准信'，或是'我的线人出城了'，诸如此类。"

"兴许哪里卡住了，"医生揣测道，"看来除了等也没法子。对了，你不是要张护照吗？这个合用吗？"

他递给我的那张纸的确切措辞我已忘了，因为后来不得不销毁；那是一份普通的身份证明书，名字是亚历山大·瓦西里耶维奇·马尔科维奇，三十三岁，邮政电报总局的文书助理。上面没有贴照片；但鉴于对护照的严苛规定——包括频繁换发（某些情形除外，护照有效期一律不得超过两个月）——以及照相之难，照片正在从通行做法中淡出。

"舒拉弄来的，"医生解释道，"他一个朋友，姓马尔科夫，新近从莫斯科来，在电报局做事。一个星期后他听说妻子病重，得了特批回去。反正彼得格勒一个星期也够他受的了——莫斯科的日子好过得多——所以他没打算回来。

===Original===

Shura asked him for his passport and after Markov had got his railroad pass and paper showing he was authorized to return to Moscow, he gave it him. If they ask for it in Moscow, he will say he has lost it. He would have to have a new one anyway, since a Petrograd one is useless there. My typewriter at the hospital has the same type as this, so we altered the date a little, added ‘itch’ to the name﻿—and there you are, if you wish, a ready-made postal official.”
“What about clothing?” I said. “I don’t look much like a postal official.”
“There is something more important than that. What about military service?”
From my pocket I produced a new pamphlet on the soviet system. Opening a pocket of the uncut leaves at a certain page, I drew forth my blank exemption certificate and exhibited it to the Doctor.
“What are you, a magician?” he asked admiringly. “Or is this another gift from your friend Z.?”
“The certificates were born twins,” I said. “Zorinsky was accoucheur to the first, I to the second.”
In an hour I had filled in the blank exemption form with all particulars relating to Alexander Vasilievitch Markovitch. Tracing the signatures carefully, and inserting a recent date, I managed to produce a document indistinguishable as regards authenticity from the original, and thus was possessed of two sets of documents, one in the name of Krylenko for the benefit of Zorinsky, the other in that of Markovitch for presentation in the streets and possible registration.
Considering once more the question of uniform I recalled that at my own rooms where I had lived for years I had left a variety of clothing when last in Petrograd six or eight months previously. The question was: How could I gain admittance to my rooms, disguised as I was and with an assumed name? Furthermore, a telephone call having elicited no response, I had no idea whether the housekeeper whom I had left was still there, nor whether the apartment had been raided, locked up, or occupied by workmen. All these things I was curious to know, quite apart from obtaining clothing.
I enlisted the services of Varia as scout. Varia was the first person to whom I confided my English name, and doing it with due solemnity, and with severe cautionings that not even Stepanovna should be told, I could see that the girl was impressed with my confidence in her.

===Chinese===

舒拉向他要了那张身份证明；马尔科夫拿到铁路乘车证和准许返回莫斯科的文书之后，就把证明给了他。莫斯科那边要是查问，他就说丢了。反正他也得换新的，彼得格勒的证件在那边不管用。我医院里那台打字机的字模同这一样，所以我们把日期稍微改了改，名字后头添了个'维奇'——得，只要您愿意，一位现成的邮政官员就成了。"

"可衣服怎么办？"我说，"我这样子不太像邮政官员。"

"还有更要紧的呢。兵役怎么办？"

我从口袋里掏出一本讲苏维埃制度的新小册子，在某一页处翻开未裁的书页夹层，抽出我那张空白免役证，给医生过目。

"您是什么人，魔术师吗？"他叹服地问，"还是这又是您那位 Z 朋友的馈赠？"

"这些证件是双胞胎，"我说，"头一个的接生婆是佐林斯基，第二个的是我。"

一个钟头之内，我把空白免役表按亚历山大·瓦西里耶维奇·马尔科维奇的全部情况填妥。小心描摹签名，再填上一个近期的日子，我制成了一份在真伪上无从分辨的文件；如此一来，我手头便有了两套证件：一套是克雷连科，给佐林斯基看的；一套是马尔科维奇，供街头查验和可能的登记之用。

再把行头的问题掂量一番，我想起在自己住了多年的寓所里，六八个月前最后一次离开彼得格勒时，还留下了各式衣物。问题是：我顶着化名、一身化装，如何进得自己的家门？况且电话打过去无人接听，我全然不知留下的那位管家还在不在，寓所是遭了搜查、被封了门，还是被工人们占了。撇开取衣服不谈，这一切我都急于知道。

我征用瓦丽娅当了侦察员。瓦丽娅是我头一个吐露英国名字的人；我郑重其事地告诉了她，并严词叮嘱连斯捷潘诺夫娜也不许说。看得出，姑娘对我的这份信任深为动容。

===Original===

Armed with a brief note to my housekeeper purporting to be written by a fictitious friend of mine, and warned to turn back unless everything were precisely as I described, Varia set out on a voyage of discovery.
She returned to impart the information that the front door of the house being locked she had entered by the yard, had encountered nobody on the backstairs, and that in answer to persistent ringing a woman, whom I recognized by the description as my housekeeper, had opened the kitchen door on a short chain, and, peering suspiciously through the chink, had at first vehemently denied any acquaintance with any English people at all. On perusing the note from my nonexistent friend, however, she admitted that an Englishman of my name had formerly lived there, but she had the strictest injunctions from him to admit nobody to the flat.
Pursuing my instructions, Varia informed the housekeeper that my friend, Mr. Markovitch, had just arrived from Moscow. He was busy today, she said, and had sent her round to inquire after my affairs, but would call himself at an early opportunity.
The one article of clothing which I frequently changed and of which I had a diverse stock was headgear. It is surprising how headdress can impart character (or the lack of it) to one’s appearance. Donning my most bourgeois fur cap, polishing my leather breeches and brushing my jacket, I proceeded on the following day to my former home, entering by the yard as Varia had done and ringing at the back door. The house appeared deserted, for I saw no one in the yard, nor heard any sounds of life. When, in reply to persistent ringing, the door was opened on the chain, I saw my housekeeper peering through the chink just as Varia had described. My first impulse was to laugh, it seemed so ridiculous to be standing on one’s own backstairs, pretending to be someone else, and begging admittance to one’s own rooms by the back door.
I hadn’t time to laugh, however. The moment my housekeeper saw the apparition on the stairway she closed the door again promptly and rebolted it, and it was only after a great deal of additional knocking and ringing that at last the door was once again timidly opened just a tiny bit.
Greeting the woman courteously, I announced myself as Mr. Markovitch, close personal friend and school companion of the Englishman who formerly had occupied these rooms.

===Chinese===

瓦丽娅揣着一封写给我管家的短简——冒称出自我的一个虚构朋友之手——出发去探虚实；我叮嘱她，只要情形同我描述的有一丝一毫不合，便立即回头。

她回来了，报告说：房子前门锁着，她从院子进去，后楼梯上一个人也没碰见；按了很久门铃，一个女人——听描述正是我的管家——在门里挂着短链开了厨房门，从门缝里狐疑地张望，起初矢口否认认识任何英国人。可等读完我那位并不存在的朋友的短简，她承认从前确有一个叫我这名字的英国人住在那儿，只是他有最严的叮嘱：任何人不得放进寓所。

瓦丽娅依我的嘱咐告诉管家：我的朋友马尔科维奇先生刚从莫斯科来，今天不得空，派她先来打听打听我的事，日后定会亲自登门。

我时常更换、备货最杂的一件行头是帽子。一顶帽子能给人添上气派（或者恰恰暴露没有气派），实在惊人。第二天，我戴上最资产者气的那顶皮帽，把皮裤子擦亮，上衣刷净，前往旧居；我照瓦丽娅的样子从院子进去，按响后门的铃。房子像无人居住：院子里不见人影，也听不见一点活气。铃声按了许久，门才挂着链子开了一条缝，我看见管家正从门缝里向外张望，同瓦丽娅说的一模一样。我的头一个冲动是想笑：站在自家的后楼梯上，装成别人，从后门求进自家的门，这有多滑稽。

可我来不及笑。管家一看见楼梯上这副尊容，立刻把门关上重新上了闩；又敲又按了好半天，那扇门才又怯生生地开了窄窄一线。

我客气地向妇人问好，自称马尔科维奇先生——从前住这几间屋子的那位英国人的至交和同学。

===Original===

My friend, I said, was now in England and regretted the impossibility of returning to Russia under present conditions. I had recently received a letter from him, I declared, brought somehow across the frontier, in which, sending his greetings to Martha Timofeievna (the housekeeper), he had requested me at the earliest opportunity to visit his home and report on its condition. To reduce Martha Timofeievna’s suspicions, I assured her that before the war I had been a frequent visitor to this flat, and gave numerous data which left no doubt whatsoever in her mind that I was at least well acquainted with the arrangement of the rooms, and with the furniture and pictures that had formerly been in them. I added, of course, that on the last occasion when I had seen my friend, he had spoken of his new housekeeper in terms of the highest praise, and assured me again in his letter that I should find her good-mannered, hospitable, and obliging.
The upshot was that, though Martha Timofeievna was at first categorical in her refusal to admit anyone to the flat, she ultimately agreed to do so if I could show her the actual letter written by “Monsieur Dukes,” requesting permission for his friend to be admitted.
I told her I would bring it to her that very afternoon, and, highly satisfied with the result of the interview, I retired at once to the nearest convenient place, which happened to be the Journalist’s, to write it.
“Dear Sasha,” I wrote in Russian, using the familiar name for Alexander (my Christian name according to my new papers), “I can scarcely hope you will ever receive this, yet on the chance that you may﻿—etc.,”﻿—and I proceeded to give a good deal of imaginary family news. Toward the end I said, “By the way, when you are in Petrograd, please go to my flat and see Martha Timofeievna﻿—etc.,” and I gave instructions as to what “Sasha” was to do, and permission to take anything he needed. “I write in Russian,” I concluded, “so that in case of necessity you may show this letter to M. T. She is a good woman and will do everything for you. Give her my hearty greetings and tell her I hope to return at the first opportunity. Write if ever you can. Goodbye. Yours ever, Pavlusha.”
I put the letter in an envelope, addressed it to “Sasha Markovitch,” sealed it up, tore it open again, crumpled it, and put it in my pocket.

===Chinese===

我说，我的朋友如今在英国，眼下这种情况回不了俄国，深感遗憾。我宣称新近收到他一封信，辗转过了边境捎来的；信里向玛尔塔·季莫菲耶芙娜（管家）致意，并嘱我尽早去看看他的家，把情形报告给他。为了打消玛尔塔·季莫菲耶芙娜的疑心，我让她确信战前我是这寓所的常客，又报出种种细节，令她毫不怀疑我至少对房间的格局、原先的家具和挂画了如指掌。当然，我还添上一句：上次见到我这位朋友时，他对新管家赞不绝口，信里又向我担保，说我将发现她有礼、好客、殷勤。

结果是：玛尔塔·季莫菲耶芙娜起初一口回绝，说谁也不许进寓所；但最后松了口——只要我能拿给她看"杜克斯先生"亲笔写的那封信，信里准许他的朋友进门。

我告诉她当天下午就把信带来。对这番交涉的结果大为满意的我，立刻退到最近便的落脚处——恰好在记者家——去写这封信。

"亲爱的萨沙，"我用俄语这样起头，用的是亚历山大的呢称（按我新证件，这是我的教名）。"我几乎不敢指望你能收到这封信，但万一体能收到——等等等等。"接着我编了大量家长里短的假消息。将近结尾时我写道："对了，你到彼得格勒时，请去我的寓所看看玛尔塔·季莫菲耶芙娜——等等等等。"然后交代"萨沙"该做些什么，并许他随意取用所需之物。结尾我写道："我用俄文写，以便必要时可将此信拿给玛·季一阅。她是个好人，会为你办妥一切的。请代我向她衷心问好，并告诉她，我一有机会就回来。能写信时务必来信。再会。你的永久的，帕夫卢沙。"

我把信装进信封，写上"萨沙·马尔科维奇收"，封了口，又撕开封口，揉皱，揣进口袋。

===Original===

The same afternoon I presented myself once more at my back door.
Martha Timofeievna’s suspicions had evidently already been considerably allayed, for she smiled amiably even before perusing the letter I put into her hand, and at once admitted me as far as the kitchen. Here she laboriously read the letter through (being from the Baltic provinces she spoke Russian badly and read with difficulty), and, paying numerous compliments to the author, who she hoped would soon return because she didn’t know what she was going to do about the flat or how long she would be able to keep on living there, she led me into the familiar rooms.
Everything was in a state of confusion. Many of the pictures were torn down, furniture was smashed, and in the middle of the floor of the dining-room lay a heap of junk, consisting of books, papers, pictures, furniture, and torn clothing. In broken Russian Martha Timofeievna told me how first there had been a search, and when she had said that an Englishman had lived there the Reds had prodded and torn everything with their bayonets. Then a family of working people had taken possession, fortunately, however, not expelling her from her room. But the flat had not been to their liking, and, deserting it soon after, they took a good many things with them and left everything else upside down.
Between them, the Reds and the uninvited occupants had left very little that could be of use to me. I found no boots or overclothing, but among the litter I discovered some underclothing of which I was glad. I also found an old student hat, which was exactly what I wanted for my postal uniform. I put it in my pocket and, tying the other things in a parcel, said I would send Varia for them next day.
While I was disentangling with my housekeeper’s aid the heap of stuff on the floor I came upon my own photograph taken two or three years before. For the first time I fully and clearly realized how complete was my present disguise, how absolutely different I now appeared in a beard, long hair, and glasses. I passed the photo to Martha Timofeievna.
“That is a good likeness,” I said. “He hasn’t altered one bit.”
“Yes,” she replied. “Was he not a nice man? It is dreadful that he had to go away. I wonder where he is now and what he is doing?”
“I wonder,” I repeated, diving again into the muck on the floor.

===Chinese===

当天下午，我再度现身自家的后门口。

玛尔塔·季莫菲耶芙娜的疑心显然已消了大半：还没看我递到她手里的信，她便和蔼地笑了，径直放我进了厨房。她就在那儿吃力地把信读了一遍（她是波罗的海沿省人，俄语说得糟糕，认字也费劲），对写信人赞不绝口，说盼他早点回来——她自己也不知道这寓所往后怎么办、还能在那儿住多久——然后领我进了那几间熟悉的屋子。

一切都乱作一团。许多画被扯了下来，家具砸烂了，餐室地板正中堆着一堆破烂：书、文件、画、家具，还有撕烂的衣服。玛尔塔·季莫菲耶芙娜用磕磕绊绊的俄语告诉我：先是一场搜查，她一说住的是个英国人，红党们就用刺刀把样样东西捅的捅、撕的撕。随后一户工人人家住了进来，所幸没有把她从她那间屋里撵出去。可这寓所不合他们的意，没多久就撇下走了，顺手带走不少东西，剩下的翻得底朝天。

红党加上那家不请自来的住户，两下里合起来，几乎没有给我留下什么有用的东西。靴子和外衣一件没找着，但在那堆杂物里翻出几件内衣，已令我欢喜。还找到一顶旧学生帽——正是我配邮政制服想要的。我把它揣进口袋，把其余东西打成一个包，说好第二天打发瓦丽娅来取。

我在管家的帮衬下清理地板上那堆东西时，翻出了一张我两三年前拍的照片。我这才头一回完完全全、清清楚楚地意识到：我如今的化装是何等彻底——蓄着胡子、留着长发、戴着眼镜的我，模样变得多么绝对不同。我把照片递给玛尔塔·季莫菲耶芙娜。

"这张拍得真像，"我说，"他一点儿没变。"

"可不是，"她答，"多好的一个人哪！他不得不走，真惨。也不知道他如今在哪儿、干着什么？"

"谁知道呢。"我应了一声，又埋头去扒地板上的烂摊子。

===Original===

To save my life I could not have looked at Martha Timofeievna at that moment and kept a straight face.
Failing to obtain an overcoat from the remnant of my belongings, I searched the markets and from a destitute gentleman of aristocratic mien procured a shabby black coat with a worn velvet collar. In this and my student hat I was the “complete postal official.” I adopted this costume for daytime purposes, but before every visit to Zorinsky I went to “No 5,” where I kept what few belongings I possessed, and changed, visiting Zorinsky only in the attire in which he was accustomed to see me.
As the end of January approached my suspicion that Zorinsky would not secure Melnikoff’s release grew. Once or twice he had not even mentioned the subject, talking energetically in his usual vivacious manner about other things. He was as entertaining as ever, and invariably imparted interesting political news, but if I broached the subject of Melnikoff he shelved it at once.
So I resolved, in spite of risks, to see if I could obtain through the Policeman information as to Melnikoff’s case. I had not seen the Policeman since I had returned from Finland, so I told him I had been delayed in that country and had only just come back. Without telling him who Melnikoff was, I imparted to him the data regarding the latter’s arrest, and what I had learned “through accidental channels” as to his imprisonment. I did not let him know my concern, lest he should be inclined purposely to give a favourable report, but charged him to be strict and accurate in his investigation, and, in the event of failing to learn anything, not to fear to admit it.
About a week later, when I phoned to him, he said “he had received an interesting letter on family matters.” It was with trepidation that I hurried to his house, struggling to conceal my eager anticipation as I mounted the stairs, followed by the gaze of the leering Chinaman.
The little Policeman held a thin strip of paper in his hand.
“Dmitri Dmitrievitch Melnikoff,” he read. “Real name Nicholas Nicholaievitch N﻿—﻿—?”
“Yes,” I said.
“He was shot between the 15th and 20th of January,” said the Policeman.

===Chinese===

这时候，打死我也没法正眼看玛尔塔·季莫菲耶芙娜还得绷住脸。

行李残余里既没捞着一件大衣，我便去逛各个市场，从一位落魄而有贵族气派的绅士手里，置办了一件磨掉了绒领的黑呢旧大衣。配上这大衣和那顶学生帽，我就是个"十足完整的邮政官员"了。这套装束我白天穿用；但每次去佐林斯基家之前，我都先回"五号"——我那点儿家当都存放在那儿——换上装，只在佐林斯基看惯的那身行头里登门。

一月将尽，我那点疑心越来越重：佐林斯基怕是办不成梅利尼科夫的获释了。有一两回他索性绝口不提此事，照旧兴致勃勃、滔滔不绝地讲别的。他还是一如既往地有趣，政治消息总是灵通；可我一提梅利尼科夫，他便立即岔开。

于是我横下心冒险一试，看能否通过"警察"打听到梅利尼科夫案子的消息。从芬兰回来后我还没见过"警察"，便告诉他我在芬兰耽搁了，刚刚回来。我没有告诉他梅利尼科夫是谁，只把此人被捕的种种资料，连同我"从偶然的渠道"听来的关押情况讲给他。我没有流露自己的关切，生怕他存心报个吉利；只嘱咐他查访务必严格、确实，若是一无所获，也不必讳言。

约莫一星期后，我给他打电话，他说"收到了一封家事方面有意思的信"。我提着一颗心赶去他家；上楼时竭力按捺住焦灼的期盼，身后跟着那个斜眼觑人的中国人的目光。

小个子"警察"手里捏着一张薄薄的纸条。

"德米特里·德米特里耶维奇·梅利尼科夫，"他念道，"真名尼古拉·尼古拉耶维奇·N——？"

"是的。"我说。

"他在一月十五到二十日之间被枪决了。""警察"说。

## IV Meshes / 四 罗网
===Original===

“Why, yes, Maria!” I exclaimed, “the way Mrs. Marsh bore up was just wonderful to see! Twelve miles in deep snow, heavy marching through thickets and scrub, over ditches and dykes, stumps and pitfalls, with never a word of complaint, as though it were a picnic! You’d never have dreamt she was just out of prison.”
“Yes, of course,” said Maria, proudly, “that would be just like her. And where is she now, Ivan Ilitch?”
“On the way to England, I guess.”
I was back again in Red Petrograd after a brief stay in Finland. That little country was supposed to be the headquarters of the Russian counterrevolution, which meant that everyone who had a plan to overthrow the Bolsheviks (and there were almost as many plans as there were patriots) conspired with as much noise as possible to push it through to the detriment of everybody else’s. So tongues wagged fast and viciously, and any old cock-and-bull story about anybody else was readily believed, circulated, and shouted abroad. You got it published if you could, and if you couldn’t (the papers, after all, had to draw the line somewhere), then you printed it yourself in the form of a libellous pamphlet. I felt a good deal safer in Petrograd, where I was thrown entirely on my own resources, than in Helsingfors, where the appearance of a stranger in a café or restaurant in almost anybody’s company was sufficient to set the puppets of a rival faction in commotion, like an ant-nest when a stone is dropped on it.
So I hid, stayed at a room in a private house, bought my own food or frequented insignificant restaurants, and was glad when I was given some money for expenses and could return to my friends Maria, Stepanovna, the Journalist, and others in Petrograd.
“How did you get back here, Ivan Ilitch?”
“Same old way, Maria. Black night. Frozen river. Deep snow. Everything around﻿—bushes, trees, meadows﻿—still and grey-blue in the starlight. Finnish patrols kept guard as before﻿—lent me a white sheet, too, to wrap myself up in. Sort of cloak of invisibility, like in the fairy tales. So while the Finns watched through the bushes, I shuffled across the river, looking like Caesar’s ghost.”
Maria was fascinated. “And did nobody see you?”
“Nobody, Maria. To make a good story I should have knocked at the door of the Red patrol and announced myself as the spirit of His Late Imperial Majesty, returned to wreak vengeance, shouldn’t I?

===Chinese===

“可不是嘛，玛丽亚！”我嚷道，“马什太太那一股子硬劲儿，真是少见！齐膝深雪里走十二英里，在灌木丛和荆棘里开路，越沟跨堤，绕树桩躲陷坑，一声苦没叫过，倒像去野餐！你怎么也想不到她刚出监狱。”

“当然是，”玛丽亚骄傲地说，“她就是这脾气。她现在在哪儿呢，伊万·伊里奇？”

“我猜，在回英国的路上。”

我在芬兰短暂逗留后，又回到了红色彼得格勒。那个小国被当作俄国反革命的大本营，意思是：谁有个推翻布尔什维克的计划（爱国者几乎人手一份），就尽可能地敲锣打鼓去密谋推进，生怕不砸了别人的锅。于是各种舌头摇得又快又毒，谁的任何荒唐故事都有人信、有人传、有人嚷到国外去。能登报就登报；登不了（报纸总得有个限度），就自己印成诽谤小册子。在彼得格勒我反倒觉得安稳得多——虽然全凭自己单打独斗；在赫尔辛福斯，生人不管同谁在馆子里一露面，就够叫敌对派系的傀儡们炸了窝，像石头砸上的蚂蚁堆。

于是我藏起来，住在一间民房的屋子里，自己买吃的，或光顾不起眼的小馆子；等领到一笔经费、能回彼得格勒去见玛丽亚、斯捷潘诺夫娜、记者这些朋友了，我心里很高兴。

“您怎么回来的，伊万·伊里奇？”

“老路子，玛丽亚。黑夜。封冻的河。深雪。四下里——灌木、树木、草场——在星光下静静的，一片灰蓝。芬兰巡逻队照旧把着——还借了我一条白床单裹身。隐身衣，童话里那种。芬兰人隔着灌木盯着，我蹭着冰面过河，活像恺撒的鬼魂。”

玛丽亚听呆了。“就没人瞧见您？”

“没人，玛丽亚。要是想故事好听，我该去敲红军巡逻队的门，自称是先皇帝陛下的亡灵，回来报仇，是不是？

===Original===

But I didn’t. Instead of that I threw away the sheet and took a ticket to Petrograd. Very prosaic, wasn’t it? I’ll have some more tea, please.”
I found a new atmosphere developing in the city which is proudly entitled the “Metropolis of the World Revolution.” Simultaneously with the increasing shortage of food and fuel and the growing embitterment of the masses, new tendencies were observable on the part of the ruling Communist Party. Roughly, these tendencies might be classed as political or administrative, social, and militarist.
Politically, the Communist Party was being driven in view of popular discontent to tighten its control by every means on all branches of administrative activity in the country. Thus the people’s cooperative societies and trade unions were gradually being deprived of their liberties and independence, and the “boss” system under Communist bosses was being introduced. At the same time elections had to be strictly “controlled,” that is, manipulated in such a way that only Communists got elected.
As an offset to this, it was evident the Communists were beginning to realize that political “soundness” (that is, public confession of the Communist creed) was a bad substitute for administrative ability. The premium on ignorance was being replaced by a premium on intelligence and training, and bourgeois “specialists” of every calling, subject to rigid Communist control, were being encouraged to resume their avocations or accept posts with remunerative pay under the Soviet Government. Only two conditions were required, namely, that the individual renounce all claim to former property and all participation in politics. These overtures were made particularly to members of the liberal professions, doctors, nurses, matrons, teachers, actors, and artists, but also to industrial and commercial experts, and even landlords who were trained agriculturists. Thus was established a compromise with the bourgeoisie.
No people in the world are so capable of heroic and self-sacrificing labour for purely altruistic motives as a certain type of Russian.

===Chinese===

可我没敲。我把床单一扔，买了张去彼得格勒的票。够没劲的，对吧？请再给我倒点茶。”

我发现这座自豪地号称“世界革命之都”的城市里，正酝酿着一股新的空气。同食物燃料日蹙、民众怨毒日深相并行的，是当权的共产党内可察的新动向。大致说来，这些动向可分政治行政、社会和军事三类。

政治上，鉴于民怨沸腾，党正千方百计加紧控制全国各行政系统。民众的合作社和工会正逐渐被剥夺自由与独立，“老板制”——由共产党老板坐庄——正在推行。同时，选举必须严加“督管”，也就是操纵到只有共产党人当选。

与之相抵的是，共产党人显然开始意识到：政治“可靠”（即公开承认共产主义信条）替代不了行政才干。愚昧的奖赏正让位给才智与训练的奖赏，各行各业的资产阶级“专家”，在共产党的严密控制下，被鼓励重操旧业，或在苏维埃政府下就任高薪职位。条件只有两个：本人放弃对旧日财产的一切权利，并退出一切政治活动。这种拉拢特别投向自由职业者——医生、护士、管理员、教师、演员、艺术家——但也及于工商专家，甚至受过农学训练的地主。同资产阶级的妥协就此建立。

世上没有一个民族，能像某一类俄国人那样，为纯粹利他的动机做出英勇的自我牺牲的劳作。

===Original===

I remember in the summer of 1918, when the persecution of the intelligentsia was at its height, drawing attention in an official report to the remarkable fact of the large number of educated Russians who had heroically stuck to their posts and were struggling in the face of adversity to save at least something from the general wreck. Such individuals might be found at times even within the ranks of “the party,” but they cared little for the silly politics of Bolshevism and nothing whatever for the world revolution. Credit is due to the Communists at least to this extent, that they realized ultimately the value of such service to humanity, and, when they discovered it, encouraged it, especially if the credit for it accrued to themselves. The work done by heroic individuals of this type served largely to counterbalance the psychological effect of ever-increasing political and industrial slavery, and it has therefore been denounced as “treacherous” by some counterrevolutionary émigrés, and especially by those in whose eyes the alleviation of the bitter lot of the Russian people was a minor detail compared with the restoration of themselves to power.
The third growing tendency, the militarist, was the most interesting, and, incidentally, to me the most embarrassing. The stimulus to build a mighty Red army for world-revolutionary purposes was accentuated by the pressing need of mobilizing forces to beat off the counterrevolutionary, or “White,” armies gathering on the outskirts of Russia, particularly in the south and east. The call for volunteers was a complete failure from the start, except in so far as people joined the Red army with the object of getting bigger rations until being sent to the front, and then deserting at the first opportunity. So mobilization orders increased in frequency and stringency and until I got some settled occupation I had to invent expedients to keep my passport papers up to date.
My friends, the Finnish patrols, had furnished me with a renewed document better worded than the last and with a later date, so I left the old one in Finland and now keep it as a treasured relic. As a precautionary measure I changed my name to Joseph Krylenko. But the time was coming when even those employees of the Extraordinary Commission who were not indispensable might be subject to mobilization.

===Chinese===

我记得 1918 年夏天，对知识界的迫害正烈，我在一份官方报告里提请人们注意一个不寻常的事实：大批受过教育的俄国人英勇地坚守岗位，在逆境中挣扎着从大崩溃里至少抢救出点什么。这样的人有时甚至在“党内”也找得到，只是他们对布尔什维克那套愚蠢的政治全无兴趋，对世界革命更是毫不在意。共产党人至少在这一点上值得称道：他们终究认识到了这类造福人类的工作的价值，一经发现，便加以鼓励——尤其当功劳能记在自己账上的时候。这类英雄人物的工作，大大抵消了政治与工业奴役日深所造成的心理影响；因此它被某些流亡的反革命者斥为“奸细行为”，尤其是那些人——在他们眼里，减轻俄国人民的苦难是小事，恢复自家的权位才是大事。

第三个日长夜大的动向是军事的，最有意思，也碰巧最叫我狼狈。为世界革命建立强大红军的鼓噪，因击退集结在俄国四郊——尤其南疆和东疆——的反革命“白”军的迫切需要而变本加厉。志愿兵的号召从头就一败涂地，只是招来这样的人：为的是领到开拔前的双份口粮，一到前线就开小差。于是动员令越发频繁、越发严苛；在我谋到正式差事之前，只得不断变着法子维持证件的时效。

我的朋友，芬兰巡逻队们，给了我一份新证件，措辞比上一份周全，日期也靠后；旧的那份我留在了芬兰，如今成了珍藏的纪念。为保险起见，我改名约瑟夫·克雷连科。但时候快到了：连契卡里那些并非缺之不可的雇员，也要被波及到动员里去。

===Original===

The Tsarist police agents, of course, and Chinese and other foreign hirelings, who eavesdropped and spied in the factories and public places, were indispensable, but the staff of clerical employees, one of whom I purported to be, might be cut down. So I had somehow to get a document showing I was exempt from military service.
It was Zorinsky who helped me out. I called him up the day after my return, eager to have news of Melnikoff. He asked me to come round to dinner and I deliberated with myself whether, having told him I expected to go to Moscow, I should let him know I had been to Finland. I decided to avoid the subject and say nothing at all.
Zorinsky greeted me warmly. So did his wife. As we seated ourselves at the dinner-table I noticed there was still no lack of good food, though Elena Ivanovna, of course, complained.
“Your health, Pavel Ivanitch,” exclaimed Zorinsky as usual; “glad to see you back. How are things over there?”
“Over where?” I queried.
“Why, in Finland, of course.”
So he knew already! It was a good thing for me that I had devoted a deal of thought to the enigmatical personality of my companion. I could not make him out. Personally, I disliked him intensely, yet he had already been of considerable service and in any case I needed his assistance to effect Melnikoff’s release. On one occasion he had mentioned, in passing, that he knew Melnikoff’s friend Ivan Sergeievitch, so it had been my intention to question the latter on the subject while in Finland, but he was away and I had seen no one else to ask. The upshot of my deliberations was that I resolved to cultivate Zorinsky’s acquaintance for my own ends, but until I knew him better never to betray any true feelings of surprise, fear, or satisfaction.
Disconcerted, therefore, as I was by his knowledge of my movements, I managed to divert my undeniable confusion into an expression of disgust.
“Rotten,” I replied with a good deal of emphasis, and, incidentally, of truth. “Absolutely rotten. If people here think Finland is going to do anything against the Bolsheviks they are mistaken. I never in my life saw such a mess-up of factions and feuds.”
“But is there plenty to eat there?” put in Elena Ivanovna, this being the sole subject that interested her.

===Chinese===

沙皇警探们当然缺不得，还有中国人和别国的雇佣暗探，在工厂和公共场所窃听窥伺；至于文员班底——我冒充的正是其中一员——是可能被裁的。所以我得设法弄到一份免服兵役的证明。

这回救我的是佐林斯基。回来第二天我就给他挂了电话，急于打听梅利尼科夫的消息。他请我去吃饭；我心里盘算：原先告诉他我要去莫斯科，如今要不要让他知道我去了芬兰？我决定避开这个话题，只字不提。

佐林斯基热络地迎我，他太太也一样。在餐桌坐定时，我注意到好菜仍旧不缺，当然，叶莲娜·伊万诺夫娜照例要抱怨。

“祝您健康，帕维尔·伊万内奇，”佐林斯基照老规矩嚷道，“欢迎回来。那边怎么样？”

“哪边？”我问。

“嗐，芬兰呗。”

原来他知道了！幸亏我对这位谜一样的同伴下过一番琢磨的功夫。我摸不透他。就私交说，我极其讨厌他；可他已经帮过不少忙，何况救梅利尼科夫还需仰仗他。有一回他顺口提起认识梅利尼科夫的朋友伊万·谢尔盖耶维奇，我本打算趁在芬兰时向后者问个究竟，偏偏他不在，旁人又无从问起。这一番斟酌的结果是：为着我自己的目的，决计继续维系同佐林斯基的交情；但在摸清他之前，绝不流露一丝真实的惊愕、恐惧或欢喜。

所以，尽管他掌握我的行踪叫我心慌，我还是把这分不容否认的慌乱，硬拗成了一副厌恶的样子。

“糟透了，”我加重语气答道——顺带说一句，也是实话，“糟透了。这边的人要是指望芬兰对布尔什维克有什么动作，那是打错了算盘。我这辈子没见过派系和宿怨搅和得这么一塌糊涂的。”

“可那边吃的管够吧？”叶莲娜·伊万诺夫娜捅进来——这是她唯一关心的题目。

===Original===

“Oh, yes, there is plenty to eat,” and to her delight and envy I detailed a comprehensive list of delicacies unobtainable in Russia, even by the theatrical world.
“It is a pity you did not let me put you across the bridge at Bielo’ostrof,” observed Zorinsky, referring to his offer to assist me in getting across the frontier.
“Oh, it was all right,” I said. “I had to leave at a moment’s notice. It was a long and difficult walk, but not unpleasant.”
“I could have put you across quite simply,” he said, “﻿—both of you.”
“Who, ‘both of us’?”
“Why, you and Mrs. Marsh, of course.”
Phew! So he knew that, too!
“You seem to know a lot of things,” I remarked, as casually as I could.
“It is my hobby,” he replied, with his crooked, cynical smile. “You are to be congratulated, I must say, on Mrs. Marsh’s escape. It was, I believe, very neatly executed. You didn’t do it yourself, I suppose?”
“No,” I said, “and, to tell the truth, I have no idea how it was done.” I was prepared to swear by all the gods that I knew nothing of the affair.
“Nor have they any idea at No. 2 Gorokhovaya,” he said. “At least, so I am told.” He appeared not to attach importance to the matter. “By the way,” he continued a moment later, “I want to warn you against a fellow I have heard Marsh was in touch with. Alexei﻿—Alexei﻿—what’s his name?﻿—Alexei Fomitch something-or-other﻿—I’ve forgotten the surname.”
The Policeman!
“Ever met him?”
“Never heard of him,” I said, indifferently.
“Look out if you do,” said Zorinsky, “he is a German spy.”
“Any idea where he lives?” I inquired, in the same tone.
“No; he is registered under a pseudonym, of course. But he doesn’t interest me. I chanced to hear of him the other day and thought I would caution you.”
Was it mere coincidence that Zorinsky mentioned the Policeman? I resolved to venture a query.
“Any connection between Mrs. Marsh and this﻿—er﻿—German spy?” I asked, casually.
“Not that I know of.” For a moment a transitory flash appeared in his eyes. “You really think Mrs. Marsh was ignorant of how she escaped?” he added.
“I am positive. She hadn’t the faintest notion.”
Zorinsky was thoughtful. We changed the subject, but after a while he approached it again.
“It is impertinent of me to ask questions,” he said, courteously, “but I cannot help being abstractly interested in your chivalrous rescue of Mrs. Marsh.

===Chinese===

“哦，吃的管够。”于是我报出一长串俄国弄不到的山珍海味——连戏剧界也无从弄到——说得她又喜又妙。

“可惜您没让我送您过别洛奥斯特洛夫桥，”佐林斯基说，提起他曾帮我越境的话头。

“嗐，没什么，”我说，“走得仓促，说走就走。一路又长又难走，倒不难受。”

“我本可以轻而易举送你们过去，”他说，“你们两个。”

“谁，‘我们两个’？”

“嗐，您和马什太太呗。”

嚯！这个他也知道！

“你知道的事可真不少，”我尽量随口说。

“业余爱好，”他答，歪嘴上挂着讥诮的笑。“马什太太的越狱，我得说，值得道贺。据我所知，办得干净利落。不是您亲自经手的吧？”

“不是，”我说，“而且说实话，怎么办成的我毫无头绪。”我准备好对着满天神佛发誓对此事一无所知。

“戈罗霍瓦亚街2号也毫无头绪，”他说，“至少我听说是这样。”他似乎并不把这事放在心上。“对了，”过了一会儿他又说，“我得提醒您提防一个人，听说马什同他有过往来。阿列克谢——阿列克谢——叫什么来着？——阿列克谢·福米奇什么什么——姓我忘了。”

是那个“警察”！

“见过吗？”

“没听说过，”我淡淡地说。

“遇上可得当心，”佐林斯基说，“他是个德国间谍。”

“知道他住哪儿吗？”我用同样的腔调问。

“不知道；他当然是用化名登记的。不过我对他没兴趋。前几天碰巧听人说起，想著提醒您一声。”

佐林斯基提起“警察”，会是巧合吗？我决计冒一问。

“马什太太同这位——呃——德国间谍，有什么干系吗？”我随口问。

“没听说。”他眼里闪过一瞬即逝的光。“您真以为马什太太不知道自己是怎么逃出来的？”他又问。

“我敢肯定。她一点影子也没有。”

佐林斯基沉吟起来。我们换了话题，可过了一阵他又绕了回来。

“我这么问未免冒昧，”他客气地说，“可令我对您那番侠义救美的义举不能不生出一点抽象的兴趋。

===Original===

I scarcely expect you to answer, but I should, indeed, be interested to know how you learned she was free.”
“Why, very simply,” I replied. “I met her quite by chance at a friend’s house and offered to escort her across the frontier.”
Zorinsky collapsed and the subject was not mentioned again. Though it was clear he had somehow established a connection in his mind between the Policeman’s name and that of Mrs. Marsh, my relief was intense to find him now on the wrong tack and apparently indifferent to the subject.
As on the occasion of my first visit to this interesting personage, I became so engrossed in subjects he introduced that I completely forgot Melnikoff, although the latter had been uppermost in my thoughts since I successfully landed Mrs. Marsh in Finland. Nor did the subject recur to mind until Zorinsky himself broached it.
“Well, I have lots of news for you,” he said as we moved into the drawing-room for coffee. “In the first place, Vera Alexandrovna’s café is rounded up and she’s under lock and key.”
He imparted this information in an indifferent tone.
“Are you not sorry for Vera Alexandrovna?” I said.
“Sorry? Why should one be? She was a nice girl, but foolish to keep a place like that, with all those stupid old fogeys babbling aloud like chatterboxes. It was bound to be found out.”
I recalled that this was exactly what I had thought about the place myself.
“What induced *you* to frequent it?” I asked.
“Oh, just for company,” he replied. “Sometimes one found someone to talk to. Lucky I was not there. The Bolsheviks got quite a haul, I am told, something like twenty people. I just happened to miss, and should have walked right into the trap next day had I not chanced to find out just in time.”
My misgivings, then, regarding Vera’s secret café had been correct, and I was thankful I had fought shy of the place after my one visit. But I felt very sorry for poor Vera Alexandrovna. I was still thinking of her when Zorinsky thrust a big blue sheet of oil paper into my hands.
“What do you think of that?” he asked.
The paper was a pen-sketch of the Finnish Gulf, but for some time I could make neither head nor tail of the geometrical designs which covered it. Only when I read in the corner the words “Fortress of Kronstadt, Distribution of Mines,” did I realize what the map really was.

我不指望您回答，不过说真的，我很想知道您是怎么得知她自由的。”

“嗐，简单得很，”我答道，“我纯属偶然在一位朋友家遇上她，便提出送她越境。”

佐林斯基哑了火，这话题就此撂下。他心里显然已把“警察”的名字同马什太太挂上了钩；见他如今走上了歧路、又显然不再萦怀，我大大松了一口气。

同初次拜访这位有趣人物那时一样，他谈起的话题叫我听得入神，竟把梅利尼科夫忘了个干净——虽然自打我把马什太太平安送到芬兰，此人就一直盘踞在我心头。直到佐林斯基自己提起，我才想起来。

“喏，我有一大堆消息给你，”我们进客厅喝咖啡时他说，“头一件：薇拉·亚历山德罗夫娜的馆子被一锅端了，她也锁了进去。”

他透这个消息的口吻漫不经心。

“你不为薇拉·亚历山德罗夫娜难过？”我说。

“难过？何必？她是个好姑娘，可开那么个地方就是蠢——一屋子老糊涂，个个像喜鹊一样大声瞎聊。早晚要露。”

我记得当初我自己对那地方的看法正是如此。

“那你自己怎么会常去？”我问。

“哦，找人做伴呗，”他答，“偶尔碰上能说话的人。我算走运没赶上。布尔什维克这回收获不小，听说抓了差不多二十个。我恰好躲过，要不是碰巧及时得知，第二天就该一头走进那口陷阱了。”

这样看来，我对薇拉那家秘密馆子的不祥预感没错；幸亏去过一回之后就再没登门。可我很为可怜的薇拉·亚历山德罗夫娜难过。我还在想着她，佐林斯基把一大张蓝色油纸塞进我手里。

“您看这个如何？”他问。

那是一幅芬兰湾的钢笔草图，可上面密密麻麻的几何图形，好半天叫我摸不着头脑。直到看见角落里写着“喀琅施塔得要塞，水雷分布”，我才明白这地图到底是什么。

===Original===

“Plan of the minefields around Kronstadt and in the Finnish Gulf,” explained Zorinsky. The mines lay in inner and outer fields and the course was shown which a vessel would have to take to pass through safely. The plan proved subsequently to be quite correct.
“How did you get hold of it?” I asked, interested and amused.
“Does it matter?” he said. “There is generally a way to do these things. That is the original. If you would like to make a copy of it, you must do so tonight. It must be returned to its locked drawer in the Admiralty not later than half-past nine tomorrow morning.”
A few days later I secured through my regular Admiralty connections, whom I met at the Journalist’s, confirmation of this distribution of mines. They could not procure me the map, but they gave a list of the latitudes and longitudes, which tallied precisely with those shown on Zorinsky’s plan.
While I was still examining the scheme of minefields my companion produced two further papers and asked me to glance at them. I found them to be official certificates of exemption from military service on the ground of heart trouble, filled up with details, date of examination (two days previously), signatures of the officiating doctor, who was known to me by name, the doctor’s assistant, and the proxy of the controlling commissar. One was filled out in the name of Zorinsky. The other was complete﻿—except for the name of the holder! A close examination and comparison of the signatures convinced me they were genuine. This was exactly the certificate I so much needed to avoid mobilization and I began to think Zorinsky a genius﻿—an evil genius, perhaps, but still a genius!
“One for each of us,” he observed, laconically. “The doctor is a good friend of mine. I needed one for myself, so I thought I might as well get one for you, too. At the end of the day the doctor told the commissar’s assistant he had promised to examine two individuals delayed by business half an hour later. There was no need for the official to wait, he said; if he did not mind putting his signature to the empty paper, he assured him it would be all right. He knew exactly what was the trouble with the two fellows; they were genuine cases, but their names had slipped his memory. Of course, the commissar’s assistant might wait if he chose, but he assured him it was unnecessary.

===Chinese===

“喀琅施塔得周围和芬兰湾内的布雷区图，”佐林斯基解释道。水雷分内外两带，图上还标出舰船安全通过必须走的航道。这张图后来证明完全准确。

“你怎么弄到手的？”我又好笑又好奇地问。

“要紧吗？”他说，“这种事总有路子。这是原件。您想抄一份，就今夜抄。明早九点半以前，它必须回到海军部那只上锁的抽屉里。”

几天后，我通过在记者家碰头的海军部老关系，证实了这份布雷分布。地图他们弄不到，但给了我一串经纬度，同佐林斯基图上标的一分不差。

我还在琢磨布雷图，同伴又掏出两份文件，请我过目。那是两份官方的免役证，事由是心脏病，内容填得齐全：检查日期（两天前）、主检医生的签名——那医生的名字我知道——还有医生助手和监签政委代理人的签名。一份填的是佐林斯基的名字。另一份万事俱备——只差持有人的姓名！我把几个签名细细比对，确信都是真的。这正是我躲避动员急需的证明；我开始觉得佐林斯基是个天才——兴许是个邪恶的天才，但毕竟是天才！

“一人一张，”他言简意赅地说，“医生是我的好朋友。我自己要一张，顺带给您也弄一张。末了，医生告诉政委的助手：还有两位因公务耽搁的，约好半小时后来检查。他说官员不必等了；要是他不介意在这张空白纸上先签个字，他担保没问题。那两个家伙什么毛病他一清二楚：都是真病号，只是名字一时想不起来了。政委助理当然愿意等也行，但他担保实在不必。

===Original===

So the commissar’s assistant signed the papers and departed. Shortly after, the doctor’s assistant did the same. The doctor waited three-quarters of an hour for his two cases. They did not arrive, and here are the exemption certificates. Will you fill in your name at once?”
What? *My name!* I suddenly recollected that I had never told Zorinsky what surname I was living under, nor shown him my papers, nor initiated him into any kind of personal confidence whatsoever. Nor had my reticence been accidental. At every house I frequented I was known by a different Christian name and patronymic (the Russian mode of address), and I felt intensely reluctant to disclose my assumed surname or show the passport in my possession.
The situation was one of great delicacy, however. Could I decently refuse to inscribe my name in Zorinsky’s presence after the various favours he had shown me and the assistance he was lending me﻿—especially by procuring me the very exemption certificate I so badly needed? Clearly it would be an offence. On the other hand, I could not invent another name and thus lose the document, since it would always have to be shown together with a regular passport. To gain time for reflection I picked up the certificate to examine it again.
The longer I thought the clearer I realized that, genuine though the certificate undoubtedly was, the plot had been laid deliberately to make me disclose the name under which I was living! Had it been the Journalist, or even the Policeman, I should not have hesitated, certainly not have winced as I did now. But it was Zorinsky, the clever, cynical, and mysterious Zorinsky, for whom I suddenly conceived, as I cast a sidelong glance at him, a most intense and overpowering repugnance.
Zorinsky caught my sidelong glance. He was lolling in a rocking-chair, with a bland expression on his misformed face as he swung forward and backward, intent on his nails. He looked up, and as our eyes met for the merest instant I saw he had not failed to note my hesitation.
I dropped into the desk-chair and seized a pen.
“Certainly,” I said, “I will inscribe my name at once. This is, indeed, a godsend.”
Zorinsky rose and stood at my side. “You must imitate the writing,” he said. “I am sorry I am not a draftsman to assist you.”

===Chinese===

于是政委助理签了字走了。紧接着，医生的助手也照办。医生等他那两位病人等了三刻钟，人没来——免役证就在这儿了。您这就把名字填上吧？”

什么？我的名字！我猛然记起：我从没告诉过佐林斯基我如今用的姓，没给他看过证件，也没同他有过任何私交层面的交心。这缄默也绝非无心。我每到一户常去的住家，用的名字和父称（俄国式的称呼）都不一样；要我交出化名的姓、出示随身护照，我实在万分不情愿。

可这局面太微妙了。受过他这么多好处、正靠他帮忙——尤其这张雪中送炭的免役证——我岂能当着他的面体面地拒绝填名？明摆着是失礼。可我也不能另造一个名字、白白作废这张证——因为它随时要同正本护照一并出示。为了争取思考的余地，我拿起免役证又端详了一遍。

越想越清楚：这张证尽管确凿是真，这局却是专为逼我说出化名而设的圈套！换作记者、甚至那个“警察”，我都不会迟疑，更不会像此刻这样心里发紧。可这是佐林斯基——精明、愤世、谜一样的佐林斯基；我斜眼瞟他时，心里蓦地涌起一股极强烈的、压倒性的厌恶。

佐林斯基接住了我这一瞟。他半躺在一把摇椅里，那张走形的脸上神情和悦，一前一后地晃，只顾端详自己的指甲。他抬起头，我们目光相接的一瞬，我看出我的迟疑没能逃过他。

我一屁股坐到书桌前，抓起一支笔。

“当然，”我说，“我这就填。这真是天上掉下来的宝贝。”

佐林斯基起身站到我身边。“笔迹得仿一仿，”他说，“可惜我不是绘图员，帮不上您的忙。”

===Original===

I substituted a pencil for the pen and began to draw my name in outline, copying letters from the handwriting on the certificate. I rapidly detected the essentials of the handwriting, and Zorinsky applauded admiringly as I traced the words﻿—“Joseph Krylenko.” When they were done I finished them off in ink and laid down the pen, very satisfied.
“Occupation?” queried my companion, as quietly as if he were asking the hour.
*Occupation!* A revolver-shot at my ear could not have startled me more than this simple but completely unexpected query! The two blank lines I took to be left for the name only, but, looking closer, I saw that the second was, indeed, intended for the holder’s business or occupation. The word *zaniatia* (occupation) was not printed in full, but abbreviated﻿—*zan.*, while these three letters were concealed by the scrawling handwriting of the line below, denoting the age “thirty,” written out in full.
I managed somehow not to jump out of my seat. “Is it essential?” I asked. “I have no occupation.”
“Then you must invent one,” he replied. “You must have some sort of passport with you. What do you show the guards in the street? Copy whatever you have from that.”
Cornered! I had put my foot in it nicely. Zorinsky was inquisitive for some reason or other to learn how I was living and under what name, and had succeeded effectually in discovering part at least of what he wanted to know. There was nothing for it. I reluctantly drew my passport of the Extraordinary Commission from my pocket in order that I might copy the exact wording.
“May I see?” asked my companion, picking up the paper. I scrutinized his face as he slowly perused it. An amused smile flickered round his crooked mouth, one end of which jutted up into his cheek. “A very nice passport indeed,” he said, finally, looking with peculiar care at the signatures. “It will be a long time before you land in the cells of No. 2 Gorokhovaya if you continue like this.”
He turned the paper over. Fortunately the regulation had not yet been published rendering all “documents of identification” invalid unless stamped by one’s house committee, showing the full address. So there was nothing on the back.
“You are a pupil of Melnikoff, that is clear,” he said, laying the paper down on the desk. “By the way, I have something to tell you about Melnikoff. But finish your writing first.”

===Chinese===

我换铅笔为钢笔，开始照着免役证上的笔迹描名字。那份笔体的要点我很快抓住；我一笔一笔描出“约瑟夫·克雷连科”几个字，佐林斯基击节赞叹。描完，我用钢笔描深，搁下笔，十分满意。

“职业？”同伴问，口气平淡得像在问几点钟。

职业！这一句简单又全然意外的发问，比耳边一声枪响还叫我惊！我原以为那两行空白只留给姓名，凑近才看清，第二行填的正是持有人的职业。职业（zaniatia）一词没印全称，只印了缩写 zan.，而这三个字母又被下一行龙飞凤舞的手写体盖住了——那是全文写出的年龄“三十”。

我总算没有从椅子上跳起来。“非填不可？”我问，“我没有职业。”

“那就编一个，”他答，“您身上总得带着某种证件吧。街上宪兵查您，您出示什么？照那上面抄。”

逼到墙角了！这一脚我踩得漂亮。佐林斯基出于某种缘由，非要探明我的生活和我用的名字，而且至少已经得手了一半。没有退路了。我不情不愿地从口袋里掏出契卡发的护照，好照抄准确的措辞。

“能看看吗？”同伴拿起那张纸。我端详着他的脸，看他慢慢地读。一丝好玩的笑在他那张歪嘴边掠过——嘴的一角直挑进腮帮。“真是份好护照，”他末了说，对那几个签名格外细看了一阵。“照这么下去，您一时半会儿还落不到戈罗霍瓦亚街2号的号子里去。”

他把纸翻了过来。幸好，要求一切“身份证明”须经房管会盖章注明详细地址、否则作废的新规还没颁布，背面空无一物。

“您是梅利尼科夫的徒弟，这瞒不了人，”他把纸放回桌上说，“对了，关于梅利尼科夫，我有话告诉您。不过先把字写完。”

===Original===

I soon inscribed my occupation of clerk in an office of the Extraordinary Commission, adding also “six” to the age to conform with my other papers. As I traced the letters I tried to sum up the situation. Melnikoff, I hoped, would now soon be free, but misgivings began to arise regarding my own position, which I had a disquieting suspicion had in some way become jeopardized as a result of the disclosures I had had to make that evening to Zorinsky.
When I had finished I folded the exemption certificate and put it with my passport in my pocket.
“Well, what is the news of Melnikoff?” I said.
Zorinsky was engrossed in *Pravda*, the official Press organ of the Communist Party. “I beg your pardon? Oh, yes﻿—Melnikoff. I have no doubt he will be released, but the investigator wants the whole 60,000 roubles first.”
“That is strange,” I observed, surprised. “You told me he would only want the second half *after* Melnikoff’s release.”
“True. But I suppose now he fears he won’t have time to get it, since he also will have to quit.”
“And meanwhile what guarantee have I﻿—have we﻿—that the investigator will fulfil his pledge?”
Zorinsky looked indifferently over the top of his newspaper.
“Guarantee? None,” he replied, in his usual laconic manner.
“Then why the devil should I throw away another 30,000 roubles on the off-chance﻿—?”
“You needn’t if you don’t want to,” he put in, in the same tone.
“Are you not interested in the subject?” I said, secretly indignant at his manner.
“Of course I am. But what is the use of getting on one’s hind-legs about it? The investigator wants his money in advance. Without it, he will certainly risk nothing. With it, he may, and there’s an end of it. If I were you I would pay up, if you want Melnikoff let out. What is the good of losing your first 30,000 for nothing? You won’t get that back, anyway.”
I thought for a moment. It seemed to me highly improbable that a rascal investigator, having got his money, would deliberately elect to put his neck in a noose to save someone he didn’t care two pins about. Was there no other means of effecting the escape? I thought of the Policeman. But with inquiries being made along one line, inquiries along a second would doubtless be detected by the first, with all sorts of undesirable complications and discoveries. An idea occurred to me.
“Can we not threaten the life of the investigator if he plays false?”

===Chinese===

我很快填上职业：契卡某办公室文员；又把年龄加上“六岁”，好同其他证件对得上。落笔时我试着通盘掂量局势。梅利尼科夫，我希望很快就能放出来；可对自身处境的不祥之感也冒了头——我隐隐不安地怀疑，那晚被迫向佐林斯基交出的东西，已经以某种方式连累了我。

写完，我把免役证折好，同护照一起放进衣袋。

“喏，梅利尼科夫有什么消息？”我说。

佐林斯基正埋头读《真理报》——共产党的官方机关报。“您说什么？哦，是——梅利尼科夫。放人是没跑的，只是侦讯官要先收齐那六万卢布。”

“怪了，”我惊讶地说，“你原先说他要等梅利尼科夫放出之后才收另一半。”

“不假。可我琢磨，他现在怕的是来不及收——他自己也开溜在即。”

“那么凭什么担保——咱们凭什么担保侦讯官会兑现？”

佐林斯基漠然地从报纸顶上瞟了我一眼。

“担保？没有，”他照例言简意赅地答道。

“那我凭什么见鬼再白扔三万卢布去赌——”

“不想赌可以不赌，”还是那个腔调，他插进来。

“你对这事不关心？”我说，暗暗恼他的做派。

“当然关心。可跳着脚嚷有什么用？侦讯官要预付。不给，他绝不肯冒险；给了，兴许肯，就这么回事。我要是你，要捞梅利尼科夫出来，就掏钱。头一个三万白扔了图什么？横竖也回不来。”

我琢磨了片刻。一个无赖侦讯官收足了钱，会为一个他半文不值的家伙把脖子伸进绞索？我看绝无此理。可还有别的营救路子吗？我想起那个“警察”。但一线的打听正在进行，再开二线，头一条线必会察觉，惹出种种不堪设想的乱子和暴露。灵光一闪。

“侦讯官要是耍花招，咱们不能拿他的命威胁他吗？”我提议。

===Original===

I suggested.
Zorinsky considered. “You mean hire someone to shoot him? That would cost a lot of money and we should be in the hands of our hired assassin as much as we are now in those of our investigator, while if he were shot we should lose the last chance of saving Melnikoff. Besides, the day after we threaten the investigator’s life he will decamp with the first thirty thousand in his pocket. Pay up, Pavel Ivanitch, pay up and take the chance﻿—that’s my advice.”
Zorinsky picked up his paper and went on reading.
What should I do? Faint though the chance seemed, I resolved to take it, as it was the only one. I told Zorinsky I would bring him the money on the morrow.
“All right,” he said, adding thoughtfully, as he laid aside the newspaper. “By the way, I think you were perhaps right about threatening the investigator’s life. Yes. It is not a bad idea. He need not know we know we are really powerless. We will tell him he is being tracked and cannot escape us. I will see what can be done about it. You are right, after all, Pavel Ivanitch.”
Satisfied at having made this suggestion, I set about to copy the map of the minefields and then retired for the night.
Not to sleep, however. For hours I paced up and down the soft carpet, recalling every word of the evening’s conversation, and trying to invent a means of making myself again independent of Zorinsky.
Would Melnikoff be released? The prospects seemed suddenly to have diminished. Meanwhile, Zorinsky knew my name, and might, for all I knew, out of sheer curiosity, be designing to discover my haunts and acquaintances. I recalled poignantly how I had been cornered that evening and forced to show him my passport.
With this train of thought I took my newly procured exemption certificate from my pocket and examined it again. Yes, it certainly was a treasure. “Incurable heart trouble”﻿—that meant permanent exemption. With this and my passport, I considered, I might with comparative safety even register myself and take regular rooms somewhere on the outskirts of the town. However, I resolved I would not do that as long as I could conveniently live in the centre of the city, moving about from house to house.
The only thing I did not like about my new “document” was its patent newness. I have never yet seen anybody keep tidy “documents” in Russia, the normal condition of a passport being the verge of dissolution.

===Chinese===

佐林斯基想了想。“你是说雇人崩了他？那要花一大笔钱，而且咱们就落进雇来的杀手手里，同如今落在侦讯官手里一样；他一死，救梅利尼科夫的最后指望也就断了。再说，头天威胁他的命，第二天他就揣着头一个三万块溜之大吉。掏钱吧，帕维尔·伊万内奇，掏钱赌一把——这是我的忠告。”

他拾起报纸接着读。

我怎么办？胜算虽微乎其微，我决计赌这一把——这是仅有的一把。我告诉佐林斯基，明天把钱带来。

“好，”他说着撂下报纸，若有所思地补了一句：“对了，威胁侦讯官性命这一条，你想得兴许对。嗯，不是坏主意。不必让他知道咱们其实无能为力。我们就告诉他：有人盯着他，他逃不出我们的手心。我瞧瞧能怎么办。您到底是对的，帕维尔·伊万内奇。”

这一提议得到采纳，我心满意足，着手抄那份布雷图，然后告寝。

却没能入睡。一连几个钟头，我在软地毯上踱来踱去，把当晚的对话一字字回放，盘算着怎样才能重新不再仰仗佐林斯基。

梅利尼科夫放得出来吗？前景仿佛骤然暗了下去。而佐林斯基已经知道了我的名字，天晓得他会不会出于纯粹的好奇，正盘算着探我的落脚点和交往。那晚被逼到墙角、被迫出示护照的一幕，痛切地浮上心头。

想着想着，我从口袋里重新掏出那张新弄到的免役证端详。是的，这确是件宝贝。“不治的心脏疾患”——意味着永久免役。有了它加护照，我盘算，甚至可以比较安全地去登记个正式户口，在城郊租间正经营的屋子。不过我打定主意：只要还方便在市中心一户一户地挪着住，就不这么办。

这张新“证件”唯一叫我别扭的，是它簇新得刺眼。在俄国我还从没见过谁把“证件”保管得干干净净——护照的常态，是处在溃散的边缘。

===Original===

There was no need to reduce my certificate to that state at once, since it was only two days old, but I decided that I would at least fold and crumple it as much as my passport, which was only five days old. I took the paper and, folding it tightly in four, pressed the creases firmly between finger and thumb. Then, laying it on the table, I squeezed the folds under my thumbnail, drawing the paper backward and forward. Finally, the creases looking no longer new, I began to ruffle the edges.
And then a miracle occurred!
You know, of course, the conundrum: “Why is paper money preferable to coin?”﻿—the answer being, “Because when you put it in your pocket you double it, and when you take it out you find it in creases.” Well, that is what literally did occur with my exemption certificate!
