“Why at the open spot?” I queried, surprised. “Shall I not be seen there most easily of all? Why not put me across into the woods?”
“Because the woods are patrolled, and the outposts change their place every night. We cannot follow their movements. Several people have tried to cross into the woods. A few succeeded, but most were either caught or had to fight their way back. But this meadow is a most unlikely place for anyone to cross, so the Redskins don’t watch it. Besides, being open we can see if there is anyone on the other side. We will put you across just here,” he said, indicating a narrow place in the stream at the middle of the meadow. “At these narrows the water runs faster, making a noise, so we are less likely to be heard. When you get over run up the slope slightly to the left. There is a path which leads up to the road. Be careful of this cottage, though,” he added, making a cross on the paper at the extreme northern end of the meadow. “The Red patrol lives in that cottage, but at three o’clock they will probably be asleep.”
There remained only the preparation of “certificates of identification” which should serve as passport in Soviet Russia. Melnikoff had told me I might safely leave this matter to the Finns, who kept themselves well informed of the kind of papers it was best to carry to allay the suspicions of Red guards and Bolshevist police officials. We rose and passed into another of the three tiny rooms which the villa contained. It was a sort of office, with paper, ink, pens, and a typewriter on the table.
“What name do you want to have?” asked the cadaverous man.
“Oh, any,” I replied. “Better, perhaps, let it have a slightly non-Russian smack. My accent﻿—”
“They won’t notice it,” he said, “but if you prefer﻿—”
“Give him an Ukrainian name,” suggested one of the other Finns, “he talks rather like a Little Russian.” Ukrainia, or Little Russia, is the southwest district of European Russia, where a dialect with an admixture of Polish is talked.
The cadaverous man thought for a moment. “ ‘Afirenko, Joseph Ilitch,’ ” he suggested, “that smacks of Ukrainia.”
I agreed. One of the men sat down to the typewriter and carefully choosing a certain sort of paper began to write.

===Chinese===

那儿就是你渡河的地方。"

"为什么挑开阔地？"我诧异地问，"那不是最容易被人看见吗？干吗不把我送进树林那边？"

"因为树林里有巡逻，哨位每夜都换地方，我们摸不清他们的动静。有好几个人试过从树林那边过。少数成了，多数不是被抓，就是一路打回来。可这片草场，谁也不会挑这儿渡河，红党便不设防。再说，地方开阔，对岸有没有人，我们看得见。我们就在这儿送你过。"他指着草场当中河道收窄处。"河窄处水流得急，哗哗作响，我们更不容易被听见。你过去以后，朝坡上稍稍偏左跑。有一条小径通到公路。不过要当心这座小屋，"他在纸上草场最北端画了个叉，"红军巡逻队就住那屋里。不过三点钟，他们多半睡熟了。"

剩下的只差制备"身份证明书"了——在苏维埃俄国，它们就顶护照用。梅利尼科夫告诉过我，这件事尽可放心交给芬兰人；哪种证件最能消除赤卫队员和布尔什维克警官的疑心，他们消息灵通得很。我们起身进了这座别墅三间小屋中的另一间。那算是间办公室，桌上有纸、墨水、钢笔，还有一台打字机。

"你想叫什么名字？"菜色脸问。

"随便什么都行，"我答，"或许，最好带一点非俄国的味儿。我的口音——"

"他们听不出来的，"他说，"不过你既然愿意——"

"给他起个乌克兰名字吧，"另一个芬兰人提议，"他说话挺像小俄罗斯人。"乌克兰，即小俄罗斯，是欧俄的西南地区，那里说一种掺着波兰语成分的方言。

菜色脸想了想。"'阿菲连科，约瑟夫·伊里奇'，"他建议道，"这就带乌克兰味儿了。"

我同意了。一个男人在打字机前坐下，细心挑了一种纸，开始打字。

===Original===

The cadaverous man went to a small cupboard, unlocked it, and took out a box full of rubber stamps of various sizes and shapes with black handles.
“Soviet seals,” he said, laughing at my amazement. “We keep ourselves up to date, you see. Some of them were stolen, some we made ourselves, and this one,” he pressed it on a sheet of paper leaving the imprint “Commissar of the Frontier Station Bielo’ostrof,” “we bought from over the river for a bottle of vodka.” Bielo’ostrof was the Russian frontier village just across the stream.
I had had ample experience earlier in the year of the magical effect upon the rudimentary intelligence of Bolshevist authorities of official “documents” with prominent seals or stamps. Multitudinous stamped papers of any description were a great asset in travelling, but a big coloured seal was a talisman that levelled all obstacles. The wording and even language of the document were of secondary importance. A friend of mine once travelled from Petrograd to Moscow with no other passport than a receipted English tailor’s bill. This “certificate of identification” had a big printed heading with the name of the tailor, some English postage stamps attached, and a flourishing signature in red ink. He flaunted the document in the face of the officials, assuring them it was a diplomatic passport issued by the British Embassy! This, however, was in the early days of Bolshevism. The Bolsheviks gradually removed illiterates from service and in the course of time restrictions became very severe. But seals were as essential as ever.
When the Finn had finished writing he pulled the paper out of the typewriter and handed it to me for perusal. In the top left-hand corner it had this heading:

> Extraordinary Commissar of the Central Executive Committee of the Petrograd Soviet of Workers’ and Red Armymen’s Deputies.

Then followed the text:

> This is to certify that Joseph Afirenko is in the service of the Extraordinary Commissar of the Central Executive Committee of the Petrograd Soviet of Workers’ and Red Armymen’s Deputies in the capacity of office clerk, as the accompanying signatures and seal attest.

===Chinese===

菜色脸走到一个小柜前，开了锁，取出满满一盒大小形状各异的黑柄橡皮图章。

"苏维埃印章，"他说，见我目瞪口呆，笑了。"我们的东西总是与时俱进。有几枚是偷来的，有几枚是自己刻的；这一枚，"他往一张纸上一按，印出"边境站别洛奥斯特罗夫委员"几个字，"是拿一瓶伏特加从河对岸买来的。"别洛奥斯特罗夫就是河对面的俄国边境村。

带醒目印章的官方"文书"对布尔什维克当局那点初级智力有什么样的魔力，这一年早些时候我已领教够了。形形色色的盖章文书，出门时是笔大资产；而一枚彩色大印，简直就是斩关夺隘的护身符。文书写的什么、甚至用什么语言写的，都在其次。我一位朋友有次从彼得格勒去莫斯科，全本的护照就是一张付讫的英国裁缝账单。这张"身份证明"带一个印着裁缝姓名的大抬头，贴着几张英国邮票，还有一行红墨水的花体签名。他把它直戳到官员们脸上晃，一口咬定这是英国使馆签发的外交护照！不过这是布尔什维克上台之初的事了。他们渐渐把文盲清出了公职，时日既久，限制变得非常严苛。但印章从来是要紧的。

芬兰人打完字，把纸从打字机里抽出来递给我过目。左上角的抬头是这样：

> 彼得格勒工人和红军人代表苏维埃中央执行委员会非常委员。

接着是正文：

> 兹证明约瑟夫·阿菲连科在彼得格勒工人和红军人代表苏维埃中央执行委员会非常委员处充任办事员，如所附签名与印章为证。

===Original===

“In the service of the Extraordinary Commission?” I gasped, taken aback by the amazing audacity of the thing.
“Why not?” said the cadaverous man coolly, “what could be safer?”
What, indeed? What could be safer than to purport to be in the service of the institution whose duty it was to hound down all, old or young, rich or poor, educated or illiterate﻿—who ventured to oppose and sought to expose the pseudo-proletarian Bolshevist administration? Nothing, of course, could be safer! *S volkami zhitj, po voltchi vitj*, as the Russians say. “If you must live amongst wolves, then howl, too, as the wolves do!”
“Now for the signatures and seal,” said the Finn. “Tihonov and Friedmann used to sign these papers, though it don’t matter much, it’s only the seal that counts.” From some Soviet papers on the table he selected one with two signatures from which to copy. Choosing a suitable pen he scrawled beneath the text of my passport in an almost illegible slanting hand, “Tihonov.” This was the signature of a proxy of the Extraordinary Commissar. The paper must also be signed by a secretary, or his proxy. “Sign for your own secretary,” said the Finn, laughing and pushing the paper to me. “Write upright this time, like this. Here is the original. ‘Friedmann’ is the name.” Glancing at the original I made an irregular scrawl, resembling in some way the signature of the Bolshevist official.
“Have you a photograph?” asked the cadaverous man. I gave him a photograph I had had taken at Viborg. Cutting it down small he stuck it at the side of the paper. Then, taking a round rubber seal, he made two imprints over the photograph. The seal was a red one, with the same inscription inside the periphery as was at the head of the paper. The inner space of the seal consisted of the five-pointed Bolshevist star with a mallet and a plough in the centre.
“That is your certificate of service,” said the Finn; “we will give you a second one of personal identification.” Another paper was quickly printed off with the words, “The holder of this is the Soviet employee, Joseph Ilitch Afirenko, aged thirty-six years.” This paper was unnecessary in itself, but two “documents” were always better than one.
It was now after midnight and the leader of the Finnish patrol ordered us to lie down for a short rest. He threw himself on a couch in the eating-room.

===Chinese===

"在……非常委员会里当差？"我倒抽一口气，被这份惊人的胆大吓了一跳。

"有何不可？"菜色脸冷冷地说，"还有什么比这更保险？"

可不是？还有什么比冒充那个机关的差役更保险的——那机关的本分，就是把一切胆敢反抗、企图揭穿这个伪无产阶级布尔什维克政权的人，不论老幼贫富、识字不识字，统统猎捕归案？当然，再没有比这更保险的了！俄国人说得好：*S volkami zhitj, po volchi vitj*（与狼共居，就得学狼嚎）。"你既然非住在狼群里不可，那你也得像狼一样嚎！"

"现在来签名盖章，"芬兰人说，"这些纸从前是季霍诺夫和弗里德曼签的。不过签得像不像不打紧，顶用的还是印章。"他从桌上几份苏维埃文件里挑出一份带两个签名的，照着描。他挑了一支合适的笔，在我那本护照正文底下，用一手几乎认不出的斜体潦草写了"季霍诺夫"。这是那位非常委员代理人的签名。文件还得由一位秘书或其代理人签署。"你自己那秘书的签名自己签吧，"芬兰人笑着把纸推给我，"这回要写得竖直些，照这样。喏，这是原件，名字叫'弗里德曼'。"我扫了一眼原件，歪歪扭扭地划了几笔，倒也有几分像那位布尔什维克官员的签名。

"有照片吗？"菜色脸问。我给了他一张在维堡拍的照片。他裁小了，贴在纸旁。然后取过一枚圆形橡皮章，在照片上盖了两下。那是枚红印，外圈的字同纸头的抬头一样；印面中间是一颗布尔什维克五角星，星心是一柄锤和一具犁。

"这是你的任职证明，"芬兰人说，"我们再给你一张个人身份证明。"另一张纸很快打了出来，文曰："持件人为苏维埃雇员约瑟夫·伊里奇·阿菲连科，三十六岁。"这张纸本身是多余的，可两份"文书"总比一份强。

此时已过午夜，芬兰巡逻队的头儿吩咐我们躺下歇一阵。他往餐室一张沙发上一躺。

===Original===

There were only two beds for the remaining four of us and I lay down on one of them with one of the Finns. I tried to sleep but couldn’t. I thought of all sorts of things﻿—of Russia in the past, of the life of adventure I had elected to lead for the present, of the morrow, of friends still in Petrograd who must not know of my return﻿—if I got there. I was nervous, but the dejection that had overcome me in the train was gone. I saw the essential humour of my situation. The whole adventure was really one big exclamation mark! *Forsan et haec olim.*﻿ ﻿…
The two hours of repose seemed interminable. I was afraid of three o’clock and yet I wanted it to come quicker, to get it over. At last a shuffling noise approached from the neighbouring room and the cadaverous Finn prodded each of us with the butt of his rifle. “Wake up,” he whispered, “we’ll leave in a quarter of an hour. No noise. The people in the next cottage mustn’t hear us.”
We were ready in a few minutes. My entire baggage was a small parcel that went into my pocket, containing a pair of socks, one or two handkerchiefs, and some dry biscuits. In another pocket I had the medicine bottle of whisky I had hidden from Melnikoff and some bread, while I hid my money inside my shirt. One of the four Finns remained behind. The other three were to accompany me to the river. It was a raw and frosty November night, and pitch dark. Nature was still as death. We issued silently from the house, the cadaverous man leading. One of the men followed up behind, and all carried their rifles ready for use.
We walked stealthily along the road the Finn had pointed out to me on paper overnight, bending low where no trees sheltered us from the Russian bank. A few yards below on the right I heard the murmur of the river stream. We soon arrived at a ramshackle villa standing on the river surrounded by trees and thickets. Here we stood stock-still for a moment to listen for any unexpected sounds. The silence was absolute. But for the noise of the water there was not a sound.
We descended to the water under cover of the tumbledown villa and the bushes. The stream was about twenty paces wide at this point. Along both banks there was an edging of ice. I looked across at the opposite side. It was open meadow, but the trees loomed darkly a hundred paces away on either hand in the background.

===Chinese===

我们剩下四个人只有两张床，我同其中一个芬兰人挤了一张。我想睡，睡不着。什么事都想——想往日的俄国，想自己眼下选定的这趟冒险生涯，想明天，想彼得格勒那些还蒙在鼓里、万不能知道我回来的朋友——如果我到得了的话。我紧张，可火车上那种沮丧已经散了。我品出了自己处境里那本质的滑稽。这整场冒险，其实就是一个大大的惊叹号！*Forsan et haec olim*（有朝一日回味）……

那两小时的休憩长得没有尽头。我怕三点钟，又盼它快点来，早死早超生。终于，一阵窸窸窣窣的脚步从邻屋过来，菜色脸的芬兰人用枪托把一个个捅醒。"起来，"他悄声说，"一刻钟后出发。别出声。隔壁屋里的人不能听见。"

几分钟我们就收拾停当。我的全部行装是一个揣得进口袋的小包：一双袜子、一两块手绢、几块干饼干。另一个口袋里装着我瞒下梅利尼科夫的那小瓶威士忌和一些面包，钱则贴身藏在衬衫里。四个芬兰人留下一个，其余三个送我到河边。那是一个阴冷苦寒的十一月的夜，漆黑一团。天地的静穆死一般。我们悄没声地出了屋，菜色脸打头，一人殿后，个个端着随时可放的步枪。

我们蹑足沿着芬兰人夜里在纸上指给我看的那条路走，在没有树木遮蔽俄岸视线的地方便把身子伏低。右手边低处几步开外，听得见河水的低语。不多时到了一座摇摇欲坠的别墅前，它立在河边，四面围着树木和灌木丛。我们在那儿一动不动站了片刻，听有没有意外声息。寂静是绝对的。除了水声，万籁无声。

我们借着破别墅和灌木的掩护下到水边。河面在这一带约有二十步宽。两岸各镶着一道冰边。我望向对岸。那边是开阔的草场，只是左右两侧百步开外的远处，黑黢黢地蹲着树影。

===Original===

On the left I could just see the cottage of the Red patrol against which the Finns had warned me.
The cadaverous man took up his station at a slight break in the thickets. A moment later he returned and announced that all was well. “Remember,” he enjoined me once more in an undertone, “run slightly to the left, but﻿—keep an eye on that cottage.” He made a sign to the other two and from the bushes they dragged out a boat. Working noiselessly they attached a long rope to the stern and laid a pole in it. Then they slid it down the bank into the water.
“Get into the boat,” whispered the leader, “and push yourself across with the pole. And good luck!”
I shook hands with my companions, pulled at my little bottle of whisky, and got into the boat. I started pushing, but with the rope trailing behind it was no easy task to punt the little bark straight across the running stream. I was sure I should be heard, and had amidstream the sort of feeling I should imagine a man has as he walks his last walk to the gallows. At length I was at the farther side, but it was impossible to hold the boat steady while I landed. In jumping ashore I crashed through the thin layer of ice. I scrambled out and up the bank. And the boat was hastily pulled back to Finland behind me.
“Run hard!” I heard a low call from over the water.
Damn it, the noise of my splash had reached the Red patrol! I was already running hard when I saw a light emerge from the cottage on the left. I forgot the injunctions as to direction and simply bolted away from that lantern. Halfway across the sloping meadow I dropped and lay still. The light moved rapidly along the river bank. There was shouting, and then suddenly shots, but there was no reply from the Finnish side. Then the light began to move slowly back towards the cottage of the Red patrol, and finally all was silent again.
I lay motionless for some time, then rose and proceeded cautiously. Having missed the right direction I found I had to negotiate another small stream that ran obliquely down the slope of the meadow. Being already wet I did not suffer by wading through it. Then I reached some garden fences over which I climbed and found myself in the road.
Convincing myself that the road was deserted, I crossed it and came out on to the moors where I found a half-built house.

===Chinese===

左手边，我勉强辨得出红军巡逻队那座小屋——芬兰人特意提醒过我。

菜色脸在灌木丛一个豁口处占了位。片刻后他转回来，说一切妥当。"记住，"他又低声叮嘱一遍，"往左偏着点跑，可——盯住那座小屋。"他朝另外两人一示意，他们从灌木丛里拖出一条船。他们没一点声响地干着活：船尾系上长绳，舱里放好一根篙，然后把船顺岸滑进水里。

"上船吧，"头儿悄声说，"用篙自己撑过去。祝你走运！"

我同伙伴们握了手，抿了一口小瓶里的威士忌，上了船。我开始撑篙；可船尾拖着长绳，要把这叶小舟笔直撑过流水，谈何容易。我笃定自己一定会被听见；船到中流那一刻的心情，我想就同一个人走上绞架前最后一段路差不多。终于到了对岸，可人要上岸，船就稳不住。跳岸时我一脚踏破了薄冰。我手脚并用地爬上岸坡。身后，小船被急急拽回了芬兰。

"快跑！"我听见河那边一声低唤。

该死，我破冰的哗啦声到底传到了红军巡逻队那里！我本来已经在拼命跑，忽见左边小屋里亮起一盏灯。我把方向什么的叮嘱全忘光了，只知道没命地躲开那盏灯笼。跑到半坡我扑倒在地，一动不动。灯光沿河岸疾速移动。有喊声，接着突然响了几枪，芬兰那边毫无回响。随后灯光开始慢慢退回红军巡逻队的小屋，终于一切复归寂静。

我伏了半晌不动，然后起身，小心翼翼地前进。因为方向没找对，我发现还得再蹚过一条小溪，它顺着草场的坡斜流而下。反正已经湿透，蹚过去也不添什么苦。随后我到了几道菜园栅栏前，翻过去，就站在公路上了。

确信路上空无一人后，我穿过去，走出荒原，在那里寻着一座盖了一半的房子。

===Original===

Here I sat down to await the dawn﻿—blessing the man who invented whisky, for I was very cold. It began to snow, and half-frozen I got up to walk about and study the locality as well as I could in the dark. At the crossroads near the station I discovered some soldiers sitting round a bivouac fire, so I retreated quickly to my half-built house and waited till it was light. Then I approached the station with other passengers. At the gate a soldier was examining passports. I was not a little nervous when showing mine for the first time, but the examination was a very cursory one. The soldier seemed only to be assuring himself the paper had a proper seal. He passed me through and I went to the ticket office and demanded a ticket.
“One first class to Petrograd,” I said, boldly.
“There is no first class by this train, only second and third.”
“No first? Then give me a second.” I had asked the Finns what class I ought to travel, expecting them to say, third. But they replied, “First, of course,” for it would be strange to see an employee of the Extraordinary Commission travelling other than first class. Third class was for workers and peasants.
The journey to Petrograd was about twenty-five miles, and stopping at every station the train took nearly two hours. As we approached the city the coaches filled up until people were standing in the aisles and on the platforms. There was a crush on the Finland Station at which we arrived. The examination of papers was again merely cursory. I pushed out with the throng, and looking around me on the dirty, rubbish-strewn station I felt a curious mixture of relief and apprehension. A flood of strange thoughts and recollections rushed through my mind. I saw my whole life in a new and hitherto undreamt-of perspective. Days of wandering Europe, student days in Russia, life amongst the Russian peasantry, and three years of apparently aimless war work all at once assumed symmetrical proportions and appeared like the sides of a prism leading to a common apex at which I stood. Yes, my life, I suddenly realized, *had* had an aim﻿—it was to stand here on the threshold of the city that was my home, homeless, helpless, and friendless, one of the common crowd. That was it﻿—*one of the common crowd*!

===Chinese===

我坐下等天亮——一面祝福那位发明威士忌的仁兄，因为我冷得很。下起雪来；我半冻僵了，起身来回踱步，就着黑暗尽力察看地形。在车站附近的岔路口，我发现几个士兵围着一堆篝火，便赶紧缩回我那座半拉子房子，一直等到天亮。然后我随着其他旅客走近车站。门口一个士兵在查护照。头一回掣出我的证件时，我不能不紧张，可查得草草。那士兵似乎只想确证纸上有个像样的印章。他放我过去，我便走到售票口，要一张票。

"一张到彼得格勒的头等。"我大着胆子说。

"这趟车没有头等，只有二等和三等。"

"没有头等？那就二等。"我原问过芬兰人我该坐几等，料想他们会说三等。谁知他们答："自然是头等。"——非常委员会的雇员竟坐头等以外的车厢，岂不奇怪。三等是工人和农民坐的。

到彼得格勒约二十五英里，逢站必停，火车走了将近两个钟头。临近城市，车厢越挤越满，过道里、车梯上都站满了人。抵达的芬兰车站上人山人海。查证件照例草草了事。我随人流挤出来，环顾这座肮脏、垃圾狼藉的车站，心头涌起宽慰与忐忑交织的奇异滋味。万千陌生的思绪和回忆涌过脑海。我以一种全新的、前所未梦的视角看见了自己的一生。漫游欧洲的岁月、在俄国的学生时代、在俄国农民中间的生活、三年看似无目的的战时工作，忽然各归其位、匀称起来，像一架棱镜的几个面，汇聚到我此刻站立的那个顶点。是的，我突然明白，我这一生*确曾*有过一个目标——就是站在这里，站在我家园之城的门槛上，无家、无助、无友，做芸芸众生中的一员。就是它——*芸芸众生中的一员*！

===Original===

I wanted not the theories of theorists, nor the doctrines of doctrinaires, but to see what the greatest social experiment the world has ever witnessed did for the common crowd. And, strangely buoyant, I stepped lightly out of the station into the familiar streets.

===Chinese===

我要的不是理论家的理论，也不是教条主义者的教条，而是要亲眼看看这场世界前所未见的最伟大的社会实验，为芸芸众生做了些什么。于是，怀着奇异的轻快，我步履轻盈地走出车站，走进了熟悉的街道。

## VI Stepanovna / 六 斯捷潘诺夫娜
===Original===

Meanwhile, as time progressed, I made new acquaintances at whose houses I occasionally put up for a night. Over most of them I pass in silence. I accepted their hospitality as a Russian emigrant who was being searched for by the Bolsheviks, a circumstance which in itself was a recommendation. But if I felt I could trust people I did not hesitate to reveal my nationality, my reception then being more cordial still. I often reflected with satisfaction that my mode of living resembled that of many revolutionists, not only during the reign of Tsarism, but also under the present regime. People of every shade of opinion from Monarchist to Socialist-Revolutionary dodged and evaded the police agents of the Extraordinary Commission, endeavouring either to flee from the country or to settle down unobserved under new names in new positions.
One of my incidental hosts whom I particularly remember, a friend of the Journalist and a school inspector by profession, was full of enterprise and enthusiasm for a scheme he propounded for including gardening and such things in the regular school curriculum of his circuit. His plans were still regarded with some mistrust by those in power, for his political prejudices were known, but he none the less had hope that the Communists would allow him to introduce his innovations, which I believe he eventually did successfully.
The Journalist was promoted to the position of *dieloproizvoditel* of his department, a post giving him a negligible rise of salary, but in which practically all official papers passed through his hands. At his own initiative he used to abstract papers he thought would be of interest to me, restoring them before their absence could be discovered. Some of the things he showed me were illuminating, others useless. But good, bad, or indifferent, he always produced them with a sly look and with his finger at the side of his nose, as if the information they contained must be of the utmost consequence.
I persuaded him to sell off some of his books as a subsidiary means of subsistence, and we called a Jew in, who haggled long and hard. The Journalist was loth to do this, but I refused ever to give him more than the cost of his fuel, over which also I exerted a control of Bolshevist severity.

===Chinese===

其间，随着时日推移，我又结识了些新相知，偶尔也在他们家里过夜。这些人多半略过不表。我以一个正被布尔什维克通缉的俄国流亡者的身份接受他们的款待——这一层本身便是最好的引荐。但若觉得对方信得过，我并不讳言自己的国籍，那样受到的接待反而更加热络。我时常心满意足地想：我这种生活方式，同许多革命党人的何其相似——不仅在沙皇专制的年代，在眼下的政权之下也一样。从君主派到社会革命党，持各种政见的人们都在躲避、周旋于非常委员会的警探，或者设法逃出国门，或者改名换姓、在新的行当里无声无息地安顿下来。

我借宿过的主人里，有一位记得格外清楚：他是记者的朋友，职业是学督，满脑子进取精神，热衷于一项他提出的方案——把园艺之类的课程纳入他那学区的正规教学。当权者对他的计划仍怀几分戒心，因为他的政治成见人所共知；但他依然指望共产党人会容他推行这些新花样——我相信他后来果然办成了。

记者升了官，当上他那个机关的*文牍主任*（*dieloproizvoditel*），薪水上浮寥寥，但全机关的公文实际上都经他的手。他常常主动抽出一些他觉得我会感兴趣的文件，在缺失被发现之前再归还原处。他给我看的东西，有的很有启发，有的毫无用处。但无论好的孬的还是不相干的，他掏出来时总带着一脸狡黠，手指抵在鼻翼边，仿佛里面的情报必定干系重大。

我劝他卖掉一部分藏书，作个贴补生计的进项；我们唤进来一个犹太人，那人来来回回磨了半天价。记者舍不得，可我给他的钱从来不肯多过他的燃料费——在这上头，我也施行着布尔什维克式的严格管制。

===Original===

He had no conception whatever of relative values, and attached though he was to me I thought I sometimes detected in his eye a look which said with unspeakable contempt: “You miserly Englishman!”
I was unfortunate in losing Maria as a regular companion and friend. She returned to Marsh’s country farm in the hope of saving at least something from destruction, and visited town but rarely. In her place there came to live at the empty flat “No. 5” the younger of the two stable-boys, a dull but decent youth who had not joined the looters. This boy did his best no doubt to keep things in order, but tidiness and cleanliness were not his peculiar weaknesses. He could not understand why glasses or spoons should be washed, or why even in an untenanted flat tables and chairs should occasionally be dusted. Once, the tea he had made me tasting unusually acrid, I went into the kitchen to investigate the teapot. On removing the lid I found it to be half full of dead beetles.
Stepanovna continued to be a good friend. Dmitri’s regiment was removed to a town in the interior, and Dmitri, reluctant though he was to leave the capital, docilely followed, influenced largely by the new regimental commissar who had succeeded in making himself popular﻿—a somewhat rare achievement amongst commissars. Even Stepanovna admitted this unusual circumstance, allowing that the commissar was a *poriadotchny tchelovziek*, i.e. a decent person, “although he was a Communist,” and she thus acquiesced in Dmitri’s departure.
It was in Stepanovna’s company that I first witnessed the extraordinary spectacle of an armed raid by the Bolshevist authorities on a public market. Running across her in the busy Siennaya Square one morning I found she had been purchasing meat, which was a rare luxury. She had an old black shawl over her head and carried a bast basket on her arm.
“Where did you get the meat?” I asked. “I will buy some too.”
“Don’t,” she said, urgently. “In the crowd they are whispering that there is going to be a raid.”
“What sort of a raid?”
“On the meat, I suppose. Yesterday and today the peasants have been bringing it in and I have got a little. I don’t want to lose it. They say the Reds are coming.”
Free-trading being clearly opposed to the principles of Communism, it was officially forbidden and denounced as “speculation.”

===Chinese===

他对相对价值毫无概念；虽说同我亲厚，我有时仍觉得能从他的眼睛里读出一句带着说不出的轻蔑的话："你这吝啬的英国佬！"

我不幸失去了玛丽亚这位常伴左右的朋友。她回马什的乡下农场去了，指望至少从劫难里抢出点什么，很少进城。顶替她住进"五号"空寓的，是两个马倌里较小的那个——一个木讷但正派的少年，没跟着趁火打劫。这孩子想必也尽力维持，可整洁与干净偏偏不是他的长处。他不明白杯子调羹为什么要洗，也不明白即便屋里没人住，桌椅偶尔也该掸掸灰。有一回，他沏的茶尝起来苦得反常，我进厨房去查那只茶壶。揭盖一看，里面半壶死甲虫。

斯捷潘诺夫娜依旧是好朋友。德米特里的团调往内地一座城市；德米特里虽然舍不得离开京城，还是乖乖跟了去——这多半是受了新任团政委的影响，那位政委颇得人心，在政委们中间算是难得的成就。连斯捷潘诺夫娜也承认这桩稀罕事，认可那位政委是个*poriadotchny tchelovziek*（正派人），"虽说是个共产党"，于是她默许了德米特里的离去。

正是在斯捷潘诺夫娜的陪伴下，我头一回目睹了布尔什维克当局武装突查公共集市这一奇观。一天早晨，我在熙攘的谢纳亚广场撞见她，发现她一直在买肉——那是稀罕的奢侈品。她头上蒙着条旧黑头巾，臂上挋着个树皮篮。

"肉在哪儿买的？"我问，"我也去买点。"
"别去，"她急切地说，"人群里都在传，说要来突击了。"
"突击什么？"
"八成是冲肉来的。昨天今天庄稼人一直在往里运，我弄到一点。可不想丢了。都说红军要来。"

自由贸易既明摆着同共产主义的原则相抵触，便被官方明令取缔，斥为"投机倒把"。

===Original===

But no amount of restriction could suppress it, and the peasants brought food in to the hungry townspeople despite all obstacles and sold it at their own prices. The only remedy the authorities had for this “capitalist evil” was armed force, and even that was ineffective.
The meat was being sold by the peasants in a big glass-covered shed. One of these sheds was burnt down in 1919, and the only object that remained intact was an icon in the corner. Thousands came to see the icon that had been “miraculously” preserved, but it was hastily taken away by the authorities. The icon had apparently been overlooked, for it was the practice of the Bolsheviks to remove all religious symbols from public places.
I moved toward the building to make my purchase, but Stepanovna tugged me by the arm.
“Don’t be mad,” she exclaimed. “Don’t you realize, if there is a raid, they will arrest everybody?”
She pulled me down to speak in my ear.
“And what about your﻿ ﻿… ? I am sure﻿ ﻿… your papers﻿ ﻿… are.﻿ ﻿…”
“Of course they are,” I laughed. “But you don’t expect a clown of a Red guard to see the difference, do you?”
I made up my mind to get rid of Stepanovna and come back later for some meat, but all at once a commotion arose in the crowd over the way and people began running out of the shed. Round the corner, from the side of the Ekaterina Canal, appeared a band of soldiers in sheepskin caps and brown-grey tunics, with fixed bayonets. The exits from the building were quickly blocked. Fugitives fled in all directions, the women shrieking and hugging their baskets and bundles, and looking back as they ran to see if they were pursued.
Stepanovna and I stood on a doorstep at the corner of the Zabalkansky Prospect, where we could see well, and whence, if need be, we could also make good our escape.
The market place was transformed in the twinkling of an eye. A moment before it had been bristling with life and the crowded streetcars had stopped to let their passengers scramble laboriously out. But now the whole square was suddenly as still as death, and, but for a few onlookers who watched the scene from a distance, the roadway was deserted.
From fifty to sixty soldiers filed slowly into the shed and a few others, with rifles ready, hurried now and again round the outside of the building. A fiendish din arose with the entry of the soldiers.

===Chinese===

可再多的禁令也压不住它：庄稼人不顾一切阻碍，把吃食运给城里挨饿的人，按自己的价钱出售。当局对这桩"资本主义罪恶"唯一的药方就是武力，连武力也不顶用。

肉是庄稼人在一座带玻璃顶棚的大棚里卖的。1919年，这种大棚烧塌过一座，唯一完好无损的物件是角落里的一幅圣像。成千上万的人跑来看这"神迹般"幸存的圣像，当局却赶紧把它搬走了。那圣像显然是漏网了——布尔什维克的一贯做法，是把一切宗教象征从公共场所清除干净。

我朝大棚走去要买肉，斯捷潘诺夫娜一把拽住我的胳膊。
"疯了吗你！"她嚷道，"你不明白？真要突击，他们见人就抓！"

她把我拉低，凑到我耳边。
"再说你那个……？我敢说……你的证件……总归……"
"总归是好的。"我笑了。"可你总不至于指望一个跳梁小丑似的赤卫队员看出其中分别吧？"

我打定主意先甩开斯捷潘诺夫娜，回头再来买肉；可就在这时，对面人群里一阵骚乱，大棚里的人开始往外奔。街角处，从叶卡捷琳娜运河那面转出一队士兵，戴羊皮帽，穿棕灰色军服，上了刺刀。大棚的出口很快被堵死。逃散的人四下奔逃，女人们尖叫着，死死抱住篮子包袱，边跑边回头看有没有人追。

斯捷潘诺夫娜和我站在扎巴尔坎大街街口一处门阶上，那里看得真切，必要的话也溜得脱身。

市场眨眼间变了样。方才还人声鼎沸，挤满人的电车刚停下来，让乘客们费劲地往下爬。此刻整个广场却突然死一般寂静，除了几个远远围观的，路上空无一人。

五六十个士兵鱼贯而入，慢吞吞进了大棚；另有几个端着随时可放的步枪，绕着大棚外头不时巡走。士兵一进去，棚里便炸起一片鬼哭狼嚎。

===Original===

The shrieking, howling, booing, cursing, and moaning sounded as if hell itself had been let loose! It was an uncanny contrast﻿—the silent square, and the ghastly noise within the shed!
Stepanovna muttered something, but the only word I caught was “devils.” Sacks and bundles were being dragged out by the guards and hoisted on to trucks and lorries. At one door people were let out one by one after examination of their clothes and papers. The women were set at liberty, but the men, except the old and quite young boys, were marched off to the nearest Commissariat.
“What does it all mean?” I exclaimed, as we moved off along the Zabalkansky Prospect.
“Mean, Ivan Pavlovitch? Don’t you see? ‘Let’s grab!’ ‘Down with free-trading!’ ‘Away with speculators!’ That is what they say. ‘Speculation’ they call it. I am a ‘speculator,’ too,” she chuckled. “Do you think I ever got any work from the labour bureau, where I have been registered these three months? Or Varia, either, though we both want jobs. The money Ivan Sergeievitch left us is running out, but we must live somehow, mustn’t we?”
Stepanovna lowered her voice.
“So we have sold a sideboard.﻿ ﻿… Yes,” she chuckled, “we sold it to some people downstairs. ‘Speculators,’ too, I expect. They came up early in the morning and took it away quietly, and our house committee never heard anything about it!”
Stepanovna laughed outright. She thought it a huge joke.
For all your furniture, you see, was supposed to be registered and belonged not to yourself but to the community. Superfluous furniture was to be confiscated in favour of the workingman, but generally went to decorate the rooms of members of the committee or groups of Communists in whose charge the houses were placed. Sailor Communists seemed to make the largest demands. “Good morning,” they would say on entering your home. “Allow us, please, to look around and see how much furniture you have.” Some things, they would tell you, were required by the house committee. Or a new “worker” had taken rooms downstairs. He was a “party man,” that is, he belonged to the Communist Party and was therefore entitled to preference, and he required a bed, a couch, and some easy-chairs.
It was useless to argue, as some people did and got themselves into trouble by telling the “comrades” what they thought of them.

===Chinese===

尖啸、嚎叫、倒彩、咒骂、哀号，仿佛地狱整个儿放了出来！一边是死寂的广场，一边是棚里骇人的喧嚣——对比之诡谲，令人毛骨悚然。

斯捷潘诺夫娜咕哝着什么，我只听清一个词："魔鬼。"士兵们把一袋袋、一捆捆东西拖出来，装上卡车和货车。有一扇门口，人们被逐一放出，衣裳证件都查过。女人放了，男人除去老者和半大孩子，都被押往最近的军事委员部。

"这到底算怎么回事？"我们沿着扎巴尔坎大街走开时，我嚷道。
"怎么回事，伊万·帕夫洛维奇？你还看不明白？'抢哇！''打倒自由贸易！''铲除投机分子！'——他们嘴里就这么说。这叫'投机倒把'。我也是'投机分子'，"她哧哧地笑，"你以为我在劳动局登记了这三个月，他们给过我一点活干？瓦丽娅也一样，我们娘俩都想找事做。伊万·谢尔盖耶维奇留下的钱快见底了，可我们总得活着，不是吗？"

斯捷潘诺夫娜压低了嗓音。
"所以我们卖掉了一口餐具柜。……是啊，"她哧哧笑，"卖给了楼下几家的人。八成也是'投机分子'。他们一大早上来，悄没声地抬走了，咱们楼房委员会一点风声都没听着！"

斯捷潘诺夫娜放声笑起来。她觉得这事可乐透了。

要知道，你全部的家具照理都该登记注册，而且不属于你，属于社会。多余家具要没收归工人享用；可它们多半去了楼房委员会成员或受托管房的共产党小组的成员屋里，作了摆设。水兵共产党人开的口似乎最大。"早晨好啊，"他们跨进你家门便说，"请允许我们瞧瞧您有多少家具。"他们会告诉你，有几样东西楼房委员会要用。或者，楼下搬来了一个新"工人"：他是"党员"，也就是共产党党员，理应优先，需要一张床、一张睡椅、几把安乐椅。

争辩是无用的——偏有人争辩，把对"同志们"的看法直说了出来，结果自找倒霉。

===Original===

The wise and thoughtful submitted, remembering that while many of these men were out just to pocket as much as they could, there were others who really believed they were thus distributing property in the interests of equality and fraternity.
But the wily and clever would exclaim: “My dear comrades, I am delighted! Your comrade is a ‘party man’? That is most interesting, for I am intending to sign on myself. Only yesterday I put some furniture by for you. As for this couch you ask for, it is really indispensable, but in another room there is a settee you can have. And that picture, of course, I would willingly give you, only I assure you it is an heirloom. Besides, it is a very bad painting, an artist told me so last week. Would you not rather have this one, which he said was really good?”
And you showed them any rotten old thing, preferably something *big*. Then you would offer them tea and apologize for giving them nothing but crusts with it. You explained you wished to be an “idealist” Communist, and your scruples would not permit you to purchase delicacies from “speculators.”
Your visitors were not likely to linger long over your crusts, but if you succeeded in impressing them with your devotion to the Soviet regime they would be less inclined to molest a promising candidate for comradeship.
But Stepanovna possessed no such subtlety. She was, on the contrary, unreasonably outspoken and I wondered that she did not get into difficulties.
Stepanovna and Varia often used to go to the opera, and when they came home they would discuss intelligently and with enthusiasm the merits and demerits of respective singers.
“I did not like the man who sang Lensky tonight,” one of them would say. “He baaed like a sheep and his acting was poor.”
Or, “So-and-so’s voice is really almost as good as Chaliapin’s, except in the lowest notes, but of course Chaliapin’s acting is much more powerful.”
“Stepanovna,” I once said, “used you to go to the opera before the revolution?”
“Why, yes,” she replied, “we used to go to the Narodny Dom.” The Narodny Dom was a big theatre built for the people by the Tsar.
“But to the state theatres, the Marinsky opera or ballet?”
“No, that was difficult.”
“Well, then, why do you abuse the Bolsheviks who make it easy for you to go to what used to be the Imperial theatres and see the very best plays and actors?”

===Chinese===

明白事理的人忍了：他们记得，这伙人里固然有许多是出门能捞多少捞多少的，但也确有另一些人，真心相信自己这是在为平等博爱分配财产。

而机灵乖巧的人则会惊呼："亲爱的同志们，我太高兴了！你们的同志是'党员'？那可太巧了，我自己正打算入党呢。就在昨天，我还给你们留出几件家具。至于你们要的这张睡椅，实在少它不得；不过另一间屋里有一张长榻，你们尽管拿去。还有那幅画，我当然乐意奉送，只是我向您担保，那是件传家宝。再说画得很糟糕，一位画家上礼拜刚跟我说过。你们不如要这一幅，他说这幅才真好呢。"

于是你尽把些破烂陈货指给他们看，最好是件*大个头*的。然后请他们喝茶，赔不是说只有干面包皮可佐茶。你解释说，你立志做个"理想主义的"共产党人，良心不许你向"投机分子"购买精美食品。

客人们面对你的面包皮多半坐不长；可你若真让他们领教了你对苏维埃政权的忠忱，他们也就不好意思难为一位大有前途的同志候选人了。

斯捷潘诺夫娜却全无这份心机。她偏偏口快得没了分寸，我一直纳闷她怎么还没惹出祸事。

斯捷潘诺夫娜和瓦丽娅常去听歌剧，回家来便头头是道、兴致勃勃地评点各位歌手的长短。

"今晚唱连斯基的那个人我不喜欢，"两人中总有一个说，"咩咩地像只羊，做戏也差劲。"

要不就是："某某的嗓子实在快赶上夏里亚宾了，只是最低的几个音差点；不过说到做戏，夏里亚宾当然有力得多。"

"斯捷潘诺夫娜，"有一回我问，"革命以前你们常去听歌剧吗？"

"那可不，"她答，"我们常去人民宫。"人民宫是沙皇为老百姓盖的一座大剧院。

"那国家剧院呢，马林斯基的歌剧或者芭蕾？"

"不去，那可难。"

"那么，如今布尔什维克让你们轻而易举就能进从前皇家剧院的门槛，看最好的戏、最好的角儿，你们干吗还骂他们？"

===Original===

Stepanovna was stooping over the samovar. She raised herself and looked at me, considering my question.
“H’m, yes,” she admitted, “I enjoy it, it is true. But who is the theatre full of? Only schoolchildren and our ‘comrades’ Communists. The schoolchildren ought to be doing home-lessons and our ‘comrades’ ought to be hanging on the gallows. Varia and I can enjoy the theatre because we just have enough money to buy food in the markets. But go and ask those who stand in queues all day and all night for half a pound of bread or a dozen logs of firewood! How much do they enjoy the cheap theatres? I wonder, ah!”
So I said no more. Stepanovna had very decided notions of things. If she had been an Englishwoman before the war she would have been a militant suffragette.
It was at the beginning of February that I saw Stepanovna for the last time. My acquaintance with her ceased abruptly, as with other people under similar circumstances. Varia, it transpired, got into trouble through trying to communicate with Ivan Sergeievitch in Finland.
Before going to Stepanovna’s flat I always phoned and asked, “Is your father any better?”﻿—which meant, May I come and stay the night? To which she or Varia would reply, “Quite well, thank you, and he would like you to go and see him when you have time.”
On the last occasion when I called up, Stepanovna did not at once answer. Then in a voice full of indecision she stammered, “I don’t know﻿—I think﻿—I will ask﻿—please wait a moment.” I waited and could hear she had not left the telephone. At last she continued tremblingly, “No, he is no better, he is very bad indeed﻿—dying.” There was a pause. “I am going to see him,” she went on, stammering all the time, “at eleven o’clock tomorrow morning, do﻿—do you understand?”
“Yes,” I said; “I will go too and wait for you.”
Wondering if we had understood each other, I stationed myself at the corner of the street a little before eleven, and watched from a distance the entrance to Stepanovna’s house. One glance, when she came out, satisfied her I was there. Walking off in the other direction, she followed Kazanskaya Street, only once looking round to make sure I was behind, and, reaching the Kazan Cathedral, entered it. I found her in a dark corner to the right.
“Varia is arrested,” she said, in great distress. “You must come to our flat no more, Ivan Pavlovitch.

===Chinese===

斯捷潘诺夫娜正俯身对着茶炊。她直起腰，望着我，琢磨我的问题。

"唔，是啊，"她承认，"戏我是爱看的，这不假。可戏园子里坐满了谁？只有中小学生和我们'同志'共产党人。学生们本该在家做功课，我们那些'同志'本该吊在绞架上。我和瓦丽娅能看戏，是因为我们刚刚凑得出钱上市集买吃食。你去问问那些整日整夜排队、就为半磅面包或一打劈柴的人！他们又能享受几分廉价戏园子的乐子？我倒想问问，唉！"

我便不再说什么。斯捷潘诺夫娜对事情自有极分明的主见。她若是个战前的英国女人，准是一名战斗的妇女参政论者。

二月初，我见了斯捷潘诺夫娜最后一面。同她的交情戛然而止，同其她处在类似境况下的人一样。原来，瓦丽娅因试图同芬兰的伊万·谢尔盖耶维奇通信，惹上了事。

去斯捷潘诺夫娜寓所之前，我总先打电话问："令尊好些了吗？"——意思是：今晚能去借宿吗？她或瓦丽娅便答："好多了，谢谢您，他还盼您得空去看看他呢。"

最后那次打电话，斯捷潘诺夫娜没有立刻作答。半晌，她才结结巴巴、满心迟疑地说："我不知道——我想——我去问问——请您等等。"我等着，听得出她没离开电话。她终于颤声续道："不，他没见好，他非常不好——不行了。"停了停。"我要去看他，"她一直结巴着，"明天上午十一点，您——您明白吗？"

"明白，"我说，"我也去，在那儿等你。"

不知我们是否彼此会意，十一点差几分，我守候在街角，远远望着斯捷潘诺夫娜家的门口。她出来时一眼扫过，便确知我到了。她朝另一个方向走去，沿喀山街走，只回头看了一次，确认我跟在后面，到喀山大教堂便进去了。我在右侧一个阴暗的角落里找到她。

"瓦丽娅被捕了，"她极为痛苦地说，"您再不能上我们家来了，伊万·帕夫洛维奇。

===Original===

A messenger came from Viborg the day before yesterday and asked Varia, if she could, to get out to Finland. They went together to the Finland Station and got on the train. There they met another man who was to help them get over the frontier. He was arrested on the train and the other two with him.”
“Is there any serious charge?” I asked. “Simply running away is no grave offence.”
“They say the two men will be shot,” she replied. “But Varia only had some things she was taking to Ivan Sergeievitch’s wife.”
I tried to reassure her, saying I would endeavour to discover how Varia’s case stood, and would find some means of communication.
“I am expecting a search,” she went on, “but of course I have made preparations. Maybe we shall meet again some day, Ivan Pavlovitch. I hope so.”
I felt very sorry for poor Stepanovna in her trouble. She was a fine type of woman in her way, though her views on things were often crude. But it must be remembered that she was only a peasant. As I was crossing the threshold of the cathedral, something moved me to turn back for a moment, and I saw Stepanovna shuffle up to the altar and fall on her knees. Then I came away.
I was resolved to get the Policeman on the job at once to find out the circumstances of Varia’s case, which I felt sure could not be serious. But I was not destined to make this investigation. I never saw either Varia or Stepanovna again, nor was it possible for me to discover what ultimately became of them. Tossed hither and thither by the caprice of circumstance, I found myself shortly afterwards suddenly placed in a novel and unexpected situation, of which and its results, if the reader have patience to read a little further, he will learn.

===Chinese===

前天维堡来了个信使，问瓦丽娅能不能设法去一趟芬兰。她们一起到芬兰车站上了火车。车上有另一个男人接应，本要帮她们过境。那人在车上被捕，她们两个也一起被带走了。"

"罪名重吗？"我问，"光是想跑，算不得大罪。"

"他们说那两个男人要枪毙，"她答，"可瓦丽娅不过是带了点东西，捎给伊万·谢尔盖耶维奇的妻子。"

我尽力宽慰她，说我一定设法打听瓦丽娅案子的底细，再想个传递消息的路子。

"我等着他们来搜，"她接着说，"不过我当然做了准备。兴许咱们哪天还能再见，伊万·帕夫洛维奇。我盼着这样。"

可怜的斯捷潘诺夫娜遭此变故，我心里非常难过。她自有一种好女人的风骨，尽管见识常常粗直。但须记得，她不过是个农妇。跨出教堂门槛时，不知什么促使我回头望了一眼：只见斯捷潘诺夫娜挪到祭坛前，跪了下去。我便走开了。

我决计立刻让"警察"去查瓦丽娅案子的情形——我确信案情节不重。可这趟查访终究没轮到我做成。我再没见过瓦丽娅，也没见过斯捷潘诺夫娜，她们最终的结局如何，我也无从探知。命途的风浪把我抛来掷去，不久之后，我蓦然置身于一个全新而始料未及的境地——此中情由与结果，读者若有耐心再读几页，自会知晓。

## III The Green Shawl / 三 绿披巾
===Original===

I will pass briefly over the days that followed Marsh’s flight. They were concentrated upon efforts to get news of Mrs. Marsh and Melnikoff. There were frequent holdups in the street: at two points along the Nevsky Prospect all passengers were stopped to have their documents and any parcels they were carrying examined, but a cursory glance at my passport of the Extraordinary Commission sufficed to satisfy the militiamen’s curiosity.
I studied all the Soviet literature I had time to devour, attended public meetings, and slept in turn at the homes of my new acquaintances, making it a rule, however, never to mention anywhere the secret of other night-haunts.
The meetings I attended were all Communist meetings, at each of which the same banal propagandist phraseology was untiringly reeled off. The vulgar violence of Bolshevist rhetoric and the triumphant inaccuracy of statement due to the prohibition of criticism soon became wearisome. In vain I sought meetings for discussion, or where the people’s point of view would be expressed: freedom of speech granted by the revolution had come to mean freedom for Bolshevist speech only and prison for any other. Some of the meetings, however, were interesting, especially when a prominent leader such as Trotsky, Zinoviev, or Lunacharsky spoke, for the unrivalled powers of speech of a few of the leading Bolsheviks, who possess in a marked degree “the fatal gift of eloquence,” had an almost irresistible attraction.
During these days also I cultivated the friendship of the ex-Journalist, whom, despite his timidity, I found to be a man of taste and culture. He had an extensive library in several languages, and spent his leisure hours writing (if I remember rightly) a treatise on philosophy, which, for some reason or other, he was convinced would be regarded as “counterrevolutionary” and kept locked up and hidden under a lot of books in a closet. I tried to persuade him of the contrary and urged him even to take his manuscript to the department of education, in the hope that someone of the less virulent type there might be impressed with the work and obtain for him concessions as regards leisure and rations.
When I visited him the day after Marsh’s flight I found him, still wrapped in his green coat, running feverishly from stove to stove poking and coaxing the newly lit fires.

===Chinese===

马什（Marsh）出逃后的那几天，我只想一笔带过。全部心思都用在打听马什太太和梅利尼科夫（Melnikoff）的消息上。街上拦路检查频繁：涅瓦大街上有两处，所有行人都得停下，查验证件和随身包裹，不过我这本契卡（非常委员会，Extraordinary Commission）的护照，民警们扫一眼也就满足了好奇心。
我抓紧时间狼吞虎咽地读遍手边一切苏维埃书刊，出席公开集会，轮流在各位新相识家里过夜，不过立了一条规矩：任何地方都绝不提起其他夜宿处的秘密。
我参加的集会都是共产党人（Communists）的集会，每场会上照例把同一套陈腐的宣传腔调不知疲倦地倾泻一遍。布尔什维克（Bolshevist）演说那股粗野的暴戾，加上禁绝批评造成的言之凿凿的错误，很快就叫人腻烦。我想找真正讨论问题的会、能说出民众观点的会，却总是徒劳：革命赐予的言论自由，如今只意味着布尔什维克言论的自由，别的言论只配进监狱。不过有些集会还是有趣的，尤其是托洛茨基、季诺维也夫或卢那察尔斯基这样的大人物登台的时候——几位头号布尔什维克那无与伦比的口才，他们身上那种突出的“雄辩的致命天赋”，几乎有一种难以抗拒的吸引力。
那几天里我也着意结交前记者。此人尽管怯懦，我却发现是个有品位、有教养的人。他藏书甚富，兼有好几种文字，闲暇时在写（如果我没记错）一部哲学论著，出于某种缘故，他笃信这东西会被视为“反革命”，所以把它锁起来，埋在一壁橱的书底下。我极力劝他打消这个念头，甚至劝他把稿子送到教育人民委员部去，指望那边某个毒性小些的人物会赏识这部著作，替他多争取些闲暇和口粮配给。
马什出逃次日我去看他，见他仍旧裹着那件绿外套，发着热病似的在几只炉子间跑来跑去，拨火扇风，伺候刚生起来的炉火。

===Original===

He was chuckling with glee at the return of forgotten warmth and, in truly Russian style, had lit every stove in his flat and was wasting fuel as fast as he possibly could.
“What the devil is the use of that?” I said in disgust. “Where the deuce do you think you will get your next lot of wood from? It doesn’t *rain* wood in these regions, does it?”
But my sarcasm was lost on Dmitri Konstantinovitch, in whose system of economy, economy had no place. To his intense indignation I opened all the grates and, dragging out the half-burnt logs and glowing cinders, concentrated them in one big blaze in the dining-room stove, which also heated his bedroom.
“That’s just like an Englishman,” he said in unspeakable disgust as he shuffled round watching me at work. “You understand,” I said, resolutely, “this and the kitchen are the only stoves that are ever to be heated.”
Of course I found his larder empty and he had no prospect of food except the scanty and unappetizing dinner at four o’clock at the local communal eating-house two doors away. So, the weather being fine, I took him out to the little private dining-room where I had eaten on the day of my arrival. Here I gave him the biggest meal that miniature establishment could provide, and intoxicated by the unaccustomed fumes of gruel, carrots, and coffee he forgot﻿—and forgave me﻿—the stoves.
A day or two later the Journalist was sufficiently well to return to work, and taking the spare key of his flat I let myself in whenever I liked. I took him severely to task in his household affairs, and as the result of our concerted labours we saved his untidy home from degenerating completely into a pigsty. Here I met some of the people mentioned by Marsh. The Journalist was very loth to invite them, but in a week or so I had so firm a hold over him that by the mere hint of not returning any more I could reduce him to complete submission. If I disappeared for as much as three days he was overcome with anxiety.
Some people I met embarrassed me not a little by regarding me as a herald of the approaching Allies and an earnest of the early triumph of the militarist counterrevolution. Their attitude resembled at the other extreme that recently adopted by the Bolshevist Government toward impartial foreign labour delegates, who were embarrassingly proclaimed to be forerunners of the world revolution.

===Chinese===

他因为久违的温暖回转而咯咯直乐，一副地道的俄国做派，把寓所里的炉子全点着，燃料能糟蹋多快就糟蹋多快。
“见鬼，这有什么用？”我厌恶地说，“你下一茬柴火打算从哪儿变出来？这地方又不会*下*柴火，是不是？”
可我的讥讽对德米特里·康斯坦丁诺维奇（Dmitri Konstantinovitch）全然无用，在他的经济体系里，节俭没有位置。我擅自打开所有的炉门，把烧了一半的木柴和红彤彤的炭块拖出来，归拢到饭厅那只大炉子里烧成一炉旺火——那只炉子还顺带暖着他的卧室，惹得他愤慨不已。
“英国人就是这样，”他围着我转来转去、看我忙活，用难以言表的嫌弃说。“你明白，”我不容分说，“往后只许生这只炉子和厨房那只。”
不用说，我发现他的食品柜空空如也，除了四点钟在隔两个门面的公共食堂吃的那顿寡淡倒胃的饭，他没有任何别的指望。于是趁天气好，我带他到我在到达那天吃过饭的小私人餐室。我点了几样这迷你铺子拿得出的最丰盛的菜，麦粥、胡萝卜和咖啡的香气是他久违的了，把他熏得陶陶然，炉子的事也就忘了——也原谅了。
一两天后，记者身体好转，可以回去上班了，我拿了他寓所的备用钥匙，随时想来自便。他家政方面被我狠狠整饬，经我们协力操持，他那凌乱的家总算没有彻底堕落成猪圈。在这里我见到了马什提过的那几个人。记者极不情愿请他们来，可不出一个星期，我已把他攥得死死的，只消暗示一句“以后不来了”，就能叫他彻底就范。我哪怕只消失三天，他就急得坐立不安。
我遇见的有些人把我当作即将到来的协约国大军的前驱、军国主义反革命即将得胜的吉兆，让我颇为尴尬。这种态度走到另一头，倒像布尔什维克政府最近对待外国工人代表团那样——人家本持中立，却被硬说成世界革命的先驱，闹得人家下不来台。

===Original===

One evening the Journalist greeted me with looks of deep cunning and mystification. I could see he had something on his mind he was bursting to say. When at last we were seated, as usual huddled over the dining-room stove, he leaned over toward my chair, tapped me on the knee to draw my very particular attention, and began.
“Michael Mihailovitch,” he said in an undertone, as though the chairs and table might betray the secret, “I have a won‑der‑ful idea!” He struck one side of his thin nose with his forefinger to indicate the wondrousness of his idea. “Today I and some colleagues of former days,” he went on, his finger still applied to the side of his nose, “determined to start a newspaper. Yes, yes, a secret newspaper﻿—to prepare the way for the Allies!”
“And who is going to print it?” I asked, fully impressed with the wondrousness of his idea.
“The Bolshevist *Izvestia*,” he said, “is printed on the presses of the *Novoye Vremya*,2 but all the printer-men being strongly against the Bolsheviks, we will ask them to print a leaflet on the sly.”
“And who will pay for it?” I asked, amused by his simplicity.
“Well, here you can help, Michael Mihailovitch,” said the Journalist, rather as though he were conferring an honour upon me. “You would not refuse, would you? Last summer the English﻿—”
“Well, apart from technique,” I interrupted, “why are you so certain of the Allies?”
Dmitri Konstantinovitch stared at me.
“But you﻿—” he began, then stopped abruptly.
There followed one of those pauses that are more eloquent than speech.
“I see,” I said at last. “Listen, Dmitri Konstantinovitch, I will tell you a story. In the north of your vast country there is a town called Archangel. I was there in the summer and I was there again recently. When I was there in the summer the entire population was crying passionately for the Allies to intervene and save them from a Bolshevist hooligan clique, and when at last the city was occupied the path of the British general was strewn with flowers as he stepped ashore. But when I returned some weeks after the occupation, did I find jubilation and contentment, do you think? I am sorry to say I did not. I found strife, intrigue, and growing bitterness.
“A democratic government was nominally in power with the venerable revolutionist Tchaikovsky, protégé of the Allies, at its head.

===Chinese===

一天晚上，记者迎上来时满脸高深莫测的狡黠。我看得出他有一桩心事，憋着不吐不快。等我们照例在饭厅炉边偎着坐下，他朝我的椅子俯过身来，点点我的膝盖，要我格外留神，然后开了腔。
“米哈伊尔·米哈伊洛维奇，”他压低声音，仿佛桌椅会出卖这秘密似的，“我有一个妙——妙——妙极了的主意！”他拿食指敲敲瘦鼻梁的一侧，表明这主意的奇妙。“今天我和几位旧日同行，”他接着说，手指还贴在鼻梁上，“决定办一份报纸。对，对，秘密报纸——为协约国铺路！”
“那谁去印呢？”我问，完全被这主意的奇妙镇住了。
“布尔什维克的《消息报》（*Izvestia*），”他说，“就在《新时代报》（*Novoye Vremya*）2的印机上印，可排字工人个个恨透布尔什维克，我们叫他们偷偷排一张传单就是了。”
“那钱谁出？”我问，被他的天真逗乐了。
“喏，这就用得上您了，米哈伊尔·米哈伊洛维奇，”记者说，那口气倒像在赏我什么脸面。“您总不会推辞吧？去年夏天英国人不是——”
“好，技术问题且不谈，”我打断他，“您凭什么这样笃定协约国一定来？”
德米特里·康斯坦丁诺维奇直勾勾望着我。
“可您不是——”他起了个头，又猛地打住。
接着是一阵沉默，那沉默比言语更有分量。
“我明白了，”我末了说。“听着，德米特里·康斯坦丁诺维奇，我给您讲一段事。在您这泱泱大国的北方有座城，叫阿尔汉格尔斯克。夏天我在那里，前些日子又去了。夏天我在那里时，全城人都在如痴如狂地呼喊协约国来干涉，救他们出布尔什维克流氓帮之手；等那城终于被占领，英国将军登岸时，脚下铺满了鲜花。可占领后几个星期我再回去，您猜我见到的是欢腾和满意吗？很遗憾，不是。我见到的是倾轧、阴谋和与日俱增的怨毒。
“名义上掌权的是一个民主政府，首脑是协约国卵翼之下德高望重的老革命家柴可夫斯基。

===Original===

Well, one night a group of officers﻿—Russian officers﻿—summarily arrested this government established by the Allies, while the allied military leaders slyly shut one eye so as not to see what was going on. The hapless democratic ministers were dragged out of their beds, whisked away by automobile to a waiting steam launch, and carried off to a remote island in the White Sea, where they were unceremoniously deposited and left! Sounds like an exploit of Captain Kidd, doesn’t it? Only two escaped, because they happened that evening to be dining with the American Ambassador, and he concealed them in his bedroom.
“Next morning the city was startled by a sensational announcement posted on the walls. ‘By order of the Russian Command,’ it ran, ‘the incompetent government has been deposed, and the supreme power in North Russia is henceforth vested exclusively in the hands of the military commander of the occupying forces.’
“There was a hell of a hubbub, I can tell you! For who was to untangle the knot? The allied military had connived at the kidnapping by Russian plotters of a Russian government established by order of the Allies! The diplomats and the military were already at loggerheads and now they were like fighting-cocks! Finally, after two days’ wrangling, and when all the factories went on strike, it was decided that the whole proceeding had been most unseemly and undemocratic. ‘Diplomacy’ triumphed, a cruiser was despatched to pick up the wretched ministers shivering on the remote White Sea island, and brought them back (scarcely a triumphal procession!) to Archangel, where they were restored to the tarnished dignity of their ministerial pedestals, and went on trying to pretend to be a government.”
The Journalist gaped open-mouthed as I told him this story. “And what is happening there now?” he asked after a pause. “I am rather afraid to think of what is happening now,” I replied.
“And you mean,” he said, slowly, “the Allies are not﻿—?”
“I do not know﻿—they may come, and they may not.” I realized I was rudely tearing down a radiant castle the poor Journalist had built in the air.
“By why﻿—Michael Mihailovitch﻿—are you﻿—?”
“Why am I here?” I said, completing his unfinished question. “Simply because I wanted to be.”
Dmitri Konstantinovitch gasped. “You﻿—wanted to be *here*?”
“Yes,” I replied, smiling involuntarily at his incredulity.

===Chinese===

呃，一天夜里，一群军官——俄国（Russian）军官——把这个协约国亲手扶立的政府一举逮捕了，而协约国的军事首脑们乖乖闭上一只眼，只当没看见。倒霉的民主部长们被从床上拖起来，塞进汽车，风驰电掣送到一艘待命的汽艇上，运到白海上一个荒岛，往那儿一撂就算完！听着像基德船长（Captain Kidd）的勾当吧？只有两个逃脱了，因为他们那天晚上恰好在美使那里吃饭，大使把他俩藏在了自己的卧室里。
“第二天早晨，全城被墙上贴出的一张耸人听闻的布告惊呆了。布告说：‘奉俄国军方司令部令，无能之政府业已废黜，北俄最高权力自即日起专归占领军司令之手。’
“那可炸了锅了，我告诉您！这个结谁来解？协约国的军人，默许俄国阴谋家绑架了一个协约国亲手下令成立的俄国政府！外交官和军人本来已势同水火，这下更是斗鸡一般！吵了两天，等到各工厂全体罢工，总算议定：这整套行径极不体面、极不民主。‘外交’得胜，派了一艘巡洋舰去把那班在白海荒岛上瑟瑟发抖的可怜部长们接回来（也算不上什么凯旋！），送回阿尔汉格尔斯克，扶回他们那已经黯然失色的部长宝座，让他们继续假装是个政府。”
我讲这段事时，记者听得张大了嘴。“那边现在情形怎样？”半晌他问。“现在的情形，我有点怕想。”我答。
“那您的意思是，”他慢慢地说，“协约国并不——？”
“我不知道——也许来，也许不来。”我意识到，我是在粗暴地拆掉这可怜的记者在空中搭起的一座金碧辉煌的城堡。
“那为什么——米哈伊尔·米哈伊洛维奇——您却——？”
“问我为什么在这里？”我替他把没问完的问完。“就因为我想来。”
德米特里·康斯坦丁诺维奇倒吸一口气。“您——想*来*这里？”
“是的，”我不由得对他的难以置信微微一笑。

===Original===

“I wanted to be here and took the first chance that offered itself to come.” If I had told him that after mature consideration I had elected to spend eternity in Gehenna rather than in the felicity of celestial domains I should not have astonished the incredulous Journalist more.
“By the way,” I said rather cruelly, as a possibility occurred to me, “don’t go and blurt that Archangel story everywhere, or you’ll have to explain how you heard it.”
But he did not heed me. I had utterly demolished his castle of hope. I felt very sorry as I watched him. “Maybe they will learn,” I added, wishing to say something kind, “and not repeat mistakes elsewhere.”
Learn? As I looked into the Journalist’s tear-dimmed eyes, how heartily I wished they would!
---
While the journalist’s home until my arrival was only on the downward grade toward pigstydom, that of the Policeman had already long since arrived at the thirty-third degree. His rooms were in an abominable condition, and quite unnecessarily so. The sanitary arrangements in many houses were in a sad state of dilapidation, but people took urgent measures to maintain what cleanliness they could. Not so the Policeman, who lived in conditions too loathsome for words and took no steps to check the progressive accumulation of dust, dirt, and filth.
He kept a Chinese servant, who appeared to be permanently on strike, and whom he would alternately caressingly wheedle and tempestuously upbraid, so far as I could see with equal ineffect. In the nether regions of the house he occupied there lived, or frequently gathered, a bevy of Chinamen who loafed about the hall or peeped through gratings up the cellar stairways. There was also a mysterious lady, whom I never saw, but whom I would hear occasionally as I mounted the stairs, shrieking in an hysterical caterwaul, and apparently menacing the little Policeman with physical assault. Sometimes he would snarl back, and one such *scène d’amour* was terminated by a violent crash of crockery. But the affable female, whom I somehow figured as big and muscular with wild, floating hair, a sort of Medusa, had always vanished by the time I reached the top of the stairs, and the loud door-slam that coincided with her disappearance was followed by deathlike silence.

===Chinese===

“我想来，赶上头一个机会就来了。”哪怕我告诉他，我是经过深思熟虑，宁可入地狱（Gehenna）永居也不愿在天国之至福里长住，恐怕也不见得会更叫他吃惊。
“对了，”我忽然想起一件事，说得颇有些狠心，“那阿尔汉格尔斯克的故事，可别到处去嚷嚷，不然您就得解释是打哪儿听来的。”
但他没听进去。我已把他希望的城堡夷为平地。望着他，我心里很过意不去。“兴许他们会学乖，”我补了一句，想说句好话，“不至于在别处重蹈覆辙。”
学乖？望着记者泪光涔涔的眼睛，我何等衷心地盼他们学乖啊！
---
记者的家，在我到来之前不过是行将沦为猪圈的下行路上；警察（Policeman）的家却早已抵达第三十三级。他那几间屋子糟蹋得不成样子，而且全无必要。许多房子的卫生设施都破败不堪，可人们总还急急采取些措施，尽力保持能保持的清洁。警察不然，他住在难以言表的污秽里，对尘土、脏污和垢腻的层层堆积听之任之。
他雇着一名中国仆人，那人像是处于永久罢工状态，他时而甜言哄骗、时而暴跳如雷，就我看，两者同样无济于事。他住的那栋房子的底层住着、或者说常聚着一伙中国人，在门厅里晃荡，或从地窖楼梯的铁栅后窥探。还有一位神秘的女士，我从未见过，只在上楼时偶尔听见她歇斯底里地尖声哭闹，看样子还要动手打这小个子警察。有时他也回骂，有一回这样的*爱情场面*竟以瓷器砸得粉碎收场。可等我爬到楼梯顶，那位和气的女客——不知怎么我总把她想成高大魁梧、披头散发的美杜莎式人物——总是无影无踪，随着她消失的那声砰然摔门，接下去便死一般的寂静。

===Original===

The little Policeman, whose bearing was always apologetic, would accost me as though nothing were amiss, while the insubordinate Chinese servant, if he condescended to open the front door, would stand at the foot of the staircase with an enigmatical sneering grin spread over his evil features. It was altogether an uncanny abode.
Marsh had prepared the way, and the Policeman received me with profuse demonstrations of regard. I was fortunately not obliged to accept his proffered hospitality often, but when I did, it was touching to note how he would put himself out in the effort to make me as comfortable as the revolting circumstances would permit. Despite his despicable character, his cringing deceitfulness, and mealymouthed flattery, he still possessed human feelings, showed at times a genuine desire to please not merely for the sake of gain, and was sincerely and passionately fond of his children, who lived in another house.
He was excessively vain and boastful. In the course of his career he had accumulated a collection of signed photographs of notables, and loved to demonstrate them, reiterating for the fiftieth time how Count Witte said this, Stolypin said that, and So-and-so said something else. I used to humour him, listening gravely, and he interpreted my endurance as ability to venerate the great ones of the earth, and an appreciation of his illustrious connections, and was mightily pleased. He was full of grandiose schemes for the downthrow of the Red regime, and the least sign of so much as patience with his suggestions excited his enthusiasm and inspired his genius for self-praise and loquacity.
“Your predecessors, if you will allow me to say so,” he launched forth on the occasion of my first visit, “were pitifully incompetent. Even Mr. Marsh, delightful man though he was, hardly knew his business. Now *you*, Michael Ivanitch, I can see, are a man of understanding﻿—a man of quite different stamp. I presented a scheme to Marsh, for instance,” and he bent over confidentially, “for dividing Petrograd into ten sections, seizing each one in turn, and thus throwing the Bolsheviks out. It was sure of success, and yet Mr. Marsh would not hear of it.”
“How were you going to do it?”
He seized a sheet of paper and began hastily making sketches to illustrate his wonderful scheme.

===Chinese===

小个子警察素来一副赔小心的姿态，会若无其事地上来招呼我，而那不驯服的中国仆人倘若肯赏脸开大门，便站在楼梯脚下，一副邪恶的脸上挂着莫测高深的冷笑。这实在是一处邪气的居所。
马什已先为我铺了路，警察接待我时殷勤备至。所幸我不常被迫领受他那份款待，可每逢接受，又总不免感动地看到他如何竭尽所能，叫我在那令人作呕的环境里尽量舒适。这人尽管品性可鄙、谄谀成性、满口甜腻的奉承，却仍然有人的情感，时常显出并非全为图利的真心讨好，而且真挚而热烈地疼爱住在另一处房子里的几个孩子。
他虚荣透顶，好吹大牛。在他那一辈子的营生里，他攒下一匣名流签名照片，最爱拿出来演示，第五十遍念叨维特伯爵说过这个、斯托雷平说过那个、某公又说过什么。我惯于敷衍他，正襟危听，他便把我的耐性理解为敬仰世间大人物的本事、赏识他显赫交游的眼光，于是大为受用。他满脑子掀翻红色政权的宏大计划，哪怕对他的高见稍示耐心，也能点燃他的热焰，激发他自我吹擂、滔滔不绝的天才。
“您的几任前任，恕我直言，”头一回登门他就开了腔，“都无能得可怜。就连马什先生，人固然可爱，也未必懂行。而*您*，米哈伊尔·伊万内奇，我一看便知是位明白人——全然不同流品的人物。比如我曾向马什先生献上一策，”他俯身做出机密状，“把彼得格勒（Petrograd）分为十区，逐一袭取，就这样把布尔什维克轰出去。此策万无一失，可马什先生就是不听。”
“您打算怎么个做法？”
他抓过一张纸，开始匆匆画图，讲解他那妙不可言的方案。

===Original===

The capital was all neatly divided up, the chiefs of each district were appointed to their respective posts, he had the whole police force at his beck and call and about half-a-dozen regiments.
“Give but the signal,” he cried, dramatically, “and this city of Peter the Great is ours.”
“And the supreme commander?” I queried. “Who will be governor of the liberated city?”
The sanguine little Policeman smiled a trifle confusedly. “Oh, we will find a governor,” he said, rather sheepishly, hesitant to utter the innermost hopes of his heart. “Perhaps you, Michael Ivanitch﻿—”
But this magnanimous offer was mere formal courtesy. It was plain that I was expected to content myself with the secondary role of kingmaker.
“Well, if all is so far ready,” I said, “why don’t you blow the trumpets and we will watch the walls of Jericho fall?”
The little man twirled his moustache, smirking apologetically. “But, Michael Ivanitch,” he said, growing bold and bordering even on familiarity, “er﻿—funds, don’t you know﻿—after all, nowadays, you know, you get nowhere without﻿—er﻿—money, *do* you? Of course, you *quite* understand, Michael Ivanitch, that I, personally﻿—”
“How much did you tell Marsh it would cost?” I interrupted, very curious to see what he would say. He had not expected the question to be put in this way. Like a clock ticking I could hear his mind calculating the probability of Marsh’s having told me the sum, and whether he might safely double it in view of my greater susceptibility.
“I think with 100,000 roubles we might pull it off,” he replied, tentatively, eyeing me cautiously to see how I took it. I nodded silently. “Of course, we *might* do it for a little less,” he added as if by afterthought, “but then there would be subsequent expenses.”
“Well, well,” I replied, indulgently, “we will see. We’ll talk about it again some time.”
“There is no time like the present, Michael Ivanitch.”
“But there are other things to think of. We will speak of it again when﻿—”
“When﻿—?”
“When you have got Mrs. Marsh out of prison.”
The little man appeared completely to shrivel up when thus dragged brusquely back into the world of crude reality. He flushed for a moment, it seemed to me, with anger, but pulled himself together at once and reassumed his original manner of demonstrative servility.

===Chinese===

京城被他切分得整整齐齐，各区首长各就各位，全市警察都听他调遣，外加大约六个团。
“只消一声号令，”他戏剧性地喊道，“彼得大帝这座京城就是我们的了。”
“那么最高统帅呢？”我问。“解放了的城谁来当长官？”
这位踌躇满志的小警察略带窘色地笑了。“哦，长官总会找到的，”他说，颇有些忸怩，一时不敢道出心底最深的指望。“也许您，米哈伊尔·伊万内奇——”
但这番慷慨出让不过是客套。明摆着，我该安于拥立之君幕后人的次席。
“好吧，既然万事俱备，”我说，“您何不吹起号角，咱们好看着杰里科的城墙倒下来？”
小个子捻着胡子，讪笑着表示歉意。“可是，米哈伊尔·伊万内奇，”他胆子大了起来，几乎近乎放肆地说，“呃——经费，您知道的——如今这个年月，您明白，没有——呃——钱，可寸步难行，*不是吗*？当然，您*完全*明白，米哈伊尔·伊万内奇，我本人呢——”
“您跟马什先生开价多少？”我打断他，极想看看他怎么说。他没料到问题会这样问。我能像听见钟表滴答一样，听见他的脑子在飞快盘算：马什先生是否已把数目告诉了我，如今趁我这位更好说话的主顾在场，翻上一倍是否稳妥。
“我想，有100,000卢布，事情就能办成。”他试探着答，眼睛小心地觑着我。我默默点头。“当然，”他像忽然想起似的补充，“*或许*用不了那么多，可往后还有开销呢。”
“好说，好说，”我宽容地答，“再说吧。回头咱们慢慢谈。”
“今日事今日毕，米哈伊尔·伊万内奇。”
“可还有别的事要操心。等——等咱们再谈。”
“等——？”
“等您把马什太太救出监狱。”
小个子被这样粗暴地拽回赤裸裸的现实里，整个人顿时蔫了下去。我似乎看见他脸上一红，像是恼怒，但随即镇定下来，重新摆出那套热情巴结的老姿态。

===Original===

“At present we have business on hand, Alexei Fomitch,” I added, “and I wish to talk first about that. How do matters stand?”
The Policeman said his agents were busily at work, studying the ground and the possibilities of Mrs. Marsh’s escape. The whole town, he stated, was being searched for Marsh, and the inability to unearth him had already given rise to the suspicion that he had fled. In a day or two the news would be confirmed by Bolshevist agents in Finland. He foresaw an alleviation of Mrs. Marsh’s lot owing to the probable cessation of cross-examinations. It only remained to see whether she would be transferred to another cell or prison, and then plans for escape might be laid.
“Fire ahead,” I said in conclusion. “And when Mrs. Marsh is free﻿—we will perhaps discuss other matters.”
“There is no time like the present, Michael Ivanitch,” repeated the little Policeman, but his voice sounded forlorn.
---
Meanwhile, what of Melnikoff?
Zorinsky was all excitement when I called him up.
“How is your brother?” I said over the phone. “Was the accident serious? Is there any hope of recovery?”
“Yes, yes,” came the reply. “The doctor says he fears he will be in hospital some time, but the chances are he will get over it.”
“Where has he been put?”
“He is now in a private sanitarium in Gorokhovaya Street, but we hope he will be removed to some larger and more comfortable hospital.”
“The conditions, I hope, are good?”
“As good as we can arrange for under present-day circumstances. For the time being he is in a separate room and on limited diet. But can you not come round this evening, Pavel Ivanitch?”
“Thank you; I am afraid I have a meeting of our house committee to attend, but I could come tomorrow.”
“Good. Come tomorrow. I have news of Leo, who is coming to Petrograd.”
“My regards to Elena Ivanovna.”
“Thanks. Goodbye.”
“Goodbye.”
The telephone was an inestimable boon, but one that had to be employed with extreme caution. From time to time at moments of panic the Government would completely stop the telephone service, causing immense inconvenience and exasperating the population whom they were trying to placate. But it was not in Bolshevist interests to suppress it entirely, the telephone being an effectual means of detecting “counterrevolutionary” machinations.

===Chinese===

“眼下咱们手头有正事，阿列克谢·福米奇，”我补了一句，“我想先谈这个。事情办到哪一步了？”
警察说，他手下的密探正加紧活动，摸清地形和马什太太越狱的门路。他说，全城都在搜捕马什，迟迟不能归案，已使人疑心他逃了。一两天内，布尔什维克在芬兰（Finland）的探子就会证实这消息。他预计，盘讯既停，马什太太的处境会松快一些。眼下只看她会不会换牢房、换监狱，然后就可以布置越狱了。
“放手去干吧，”我末了说，“等马什太太脱身——咱们再谈别的。”
“今日事今日毕，米哈伊尔·伊万内奇，”小警察又说了一遍，可声音听着怪凄凉的。
---
这其间，梅利尼科夫那边怎样了呢？
我给佐林斯基（Zorinsky）挂电话时，他兴奋得很。
“您兄弟怎样？”我在电话里说。“伤势重吗？有希望复原吗？”
“还好，还好，”对方答。“医生说，恐怕要在医院躺一阵子，但多半能挺过去。”
“把他安置在哪儿了？”
“眼下在戈罗霍瓦亚街一家私立疗养院，我们巴望能把他转到大些、舒服些的医院去。”
“条件总还好吧？”
“就眼前的时势，能张罗到什么就算什么。眼下他住单间，吃限定饮食。您今晚不能过来一趟吗，帕维尔·伊万内奇？”
“谢谢；今晚住房委员会要开会，怕去不成，明天来吧。”
“好，明天来。我有列奥的消息，他要来彼得格勒了。”
“替我问候叶莲娜·伊万诺夫娜。”
“多谢。再见。”
“再见。”
电话是无价之宝，却须极其小心地使用。每逢恐慌时刻，政府会干脆把电话全掐断，惹得它正想安抚的民众怨声载道、倍觉不便。但彻底禁绝电话并不合布尔什维克的利益，因为电话本是侦破“反革命”阴谋的有效手段。

===Original===

The lines were closely watched, a suspicious voice or phrase would lead to a line being “tapped,” the recorded conversations would be scrutinized for hints of persons or addresses, and then the Assyrian came down like a wolf on the fold to seize books, papers, and documents, and augment the number of occupants of Gorokhovayan cells. So one either spoke in fluent metaphor or by prearranged verbal signals camouflaged behind talk of the weather or food. The “news of Leo,” for instance, I understood at once to mean news of Trotsky, or information regarding the Red army.
Zorinsky was enthusiastic when I called next day and stayed to dinner. “We’ll have Melnikoff out in no time,” he exclaimed. “They are holding his case over for further evidence. He will be taken either to the Shpalernaya or Deriabinskaya prison, where we shall be allowed to send him food. Then we’ll communicate by hiding notes in the food and let him know our plan of escape. Meanwhile, all’s well with ourselves, so come and have a glass of vodka.”
I was overjoyed at this good news. The conditions at either of the two prisons he mentioned were much better than at No. 2 Gorokhovaya, and though transference to them meant delay in decision and consequent prolongation of imprisonment, the prison regime was generally regarded as more lenient.
“By the way,” said Zorinsky, “it is lucky you have come today. A certain Colonel H. is coming in this evening. He works on the General Staff and has interesting news. Trotsky is planning to come up to Petrograd.”
Elena Ivanovna was in a bad mood because a lot of sugar that had been promised to her and her colleagues had failed to arrive and she had been unable to make cakes for two days.
“You must excuse the bad dinner tonight, Pavel Ivanitch,” she said. “I had intended to have chocolate pudding for you, but as it is there will be no third course. Really, the way we are treated is outrageous.”
“Your health, Pavel Ivanitch,” said Zorinsky, undismayed by the prospect of no third course. “Here we have something better even than chocolate pudding, haven’t we?”
He talked on volubly in his usual strain, harping back again to prewar days and the pleasures of regimental life. I asked him if he thought most of the officers were still monarchists.
“I don’t know,” he said. “I expect you’ll find they are pretty evenly divided.

===Chinese===

线路都有人监听，一句可疑的嗓音或言辞，就会招来一条线的“搭线录音”，录音被逐字推敲，搜寻人名和地址的蛛丝马迹，然后亚述人便如恶狼扑羊般降临，抄走书籍、文件、证件，给戈罗霍瓦亚的牢房再添新客。所以打电话，要么操一口流利的隐喻，要么靠事先约定的暗语，拿天气和吃食打掩护。比如“列奥的消息”，我立刻明白是托洛茨基的消息，或是关于红军（Red）的消息。
第二天我登门时，佐林斯基兴高采烈，留我吃饭。“不出几天就能把梅利尼科夫捞出来，”他叫道。“他们把他的案子压下来等补充证据。他要么解往什帕列尔纳亚，要么解往杰里亚宾斯卡亚监狱，到时我们可以送吃的进去。我们就把字条藏在线里捎进去，把越狱计划告知他。眼下我们自家一切都好，来，喝杯伏特加。”
听了这好消息我不胜欣喜。他说的两座监狱，条件都远好于戈罗霍瓦亚街二号，虽然转解意味着定案推迟、牢狱之灾相应延长，但那边的狱规一般被认为宽些。
“对了，”佐林斯基说，“您今天来得巧。今晚有位H上校要来。他在总参谋部做事，有有趣的消息。托洛茨基计划来彼得格勒。”
叶莲娜·伊万诺夫娜心情不佳，因为答应给她和同事们的一大批糖没有到货，她两天做不成点心了。
“今晚的饭不好，您得多包涵，帕维尔·伊万内奇，”她说。“本想给您做巧克力布丁的，如今第三道菜没有着落了。说真的，人家这样对待我们，太岂有此理了。”
“祝您健康，帕维尔·伊万内奇，”佐林斯基说，没有第三道菜也不见得沮丧。“咱们这个，可比巧克力布丁还好，是不是？”
他照他那套路子滔滔不绝，又扯回战前的年月和团队生活的种种乐事。我问他，军官们如今是否还多数是君主主义者。
“说不准，”他说。“我看您会发现两派不相上下。

===Original===

Very few are socialists, but a lot think themselves republicans. Some, of course, are monarchists, and many are nothing at all. As for me,” he continued, “when I joined my regiment I took the oath of allegiance to the Tsar.” (At the mention of the Tsar he stood upright and then sat down again, a gesture which astonished me, for it really seemed to be spontaneous and unfeigned.) “But I consider myself absolved and free to serve whom I will from the moment the Tsar signed the deed of abdication. At present I serve nobody. I will not serve Trotsky, but I will work with him if he offers a career. That is, if the Allies do not come into Petrograd. By the way,” he added, checking himself abruptly and obviously desirous of knowing, “do you think the Allies really will come﻿—the English, for instance?”
“I have no idea.”
“Strange. Everyone here is sure of it. But that means nothing, of course. Listen in the queues or market places. Now Kronstadt has been taken, now the Allies are in Finland, and so on. Personally, I believe they will bungle everything. Nobody really understands Russia, not even we ourselves. Except, perhaps, Trotsky,” he added as an afterthought, “or the Germans.”
“The Germans, you think?”
“Surely. Prussianism is what we want. You see these fat-faced commissars in leathern jackets with three or four revolvers in their belts? or the sailors with gold watch-chains and rings, with their prostitutes promenading the Nevsky? Those rascals, I tell you, will be *working* inside of a year, working like hell, because if the Whites get here every commissar will be hanged, drawn and quartered. *Somebody* must work to keep things going. Mark my words, first the Bolsheviks will make their Communists work, they’ll give them all sorts of privileges and power, and then they’ll make the Communists make the others work. Forward the whip and knout! The good old times again! And if you don’t like it, kindly step this way to No. 2 Gorokhovaya! Ugh!” he shuddered. “No. 2 Gorokhovaya! Here’s to you, Pavel Ivanitch!”
Zorinsky drank heavily, but the liquor produced no visible effect on him.
“By the way,” he asked, abruptly, “you haven’t heard anything of Marsh, have you?”
“Oh, yes,” I said, “he is in Finland.”
“*What!*” he cried, half rising from the table. He was livid.
“In Finland,” I repeated, regarding him with astonishment. “He got away the day before yesterday.”

===Chinese===

社会主义者极少，自命共和派的倒有一大批。当然，也还有君主主义者，更多的人什么也不是。至于我，”他接着说，“入团的时候，我向沙皇宣过效忠誓。”（一提沙皇，他霍然起立，随即又坐下。这举动让我吃惊，因为看着实在出于自发，不似作伪。）“但沙皇签署退位诏书的那一刻起，我就认为自己解除了誓约束缚，愿效忠谁就效忠谁。眼下我谁也不效忠。我不为托洛茨基效劳，但只要他给前程，我肯跟他共事。当然，前提是协约国不进彼得格勒。对了，”他猛地收住，显然很想知道，“您以为协约国当真会来吗——比如英国人？”
“毫无概念。”
“怪了。这里人人都拿得准。可这当然什么也说明不了。您去排队处、去集市上听听：一会儿说喀琅施塔得拿下了，一会儿说协约国进芬兰了，等等。我个人嘛，相信他们会把一切都搞砸。没有人真正懂俄国（Russia），连我们自己也未必。或许，”他又想想，补了一句，“只有托洛茨基懂，还有德国人。”
“德国人，您这么想？”
“当然。我们要的正是普鲁士精神。您瞧瞧那些穿皮夹克、腰里插着三四把左轮的肥脸委员？瞧瞧那些挂着金表链、戴着金戒指、带着姘头在涅瓦大街上遛弯的水兵？这帮无赖，我告诉您，不出一年就得乖乖去*干活*，往死里干，因为白军一到，每个委员都得绞死、分尸。总得*有人*干活，事情才转得动。记住我的话：先逼共产党人干活，给他们种种特权和权柄；然后再让共产党人逼其余的人干活。鞭子和笞刑万岁！好日子又回来了！谁要是不乐意，劳驾，往这边走，去戈罗霍瓦亚街二号！呸！”他打了个寒噤。“戈罗霍瓦亚街二号！敬您一杯，帕维尔·伊万内奇！”
佐林斯基酒量极大，可酒在他身上看不出一点动静。
“对了，”他忽然问，“您没听到马什的消息吧？”
“哦，听到了，”我说，“他到芬兰去了。”
“*什么！*”他喊起来，半个身子从桌上抬了起来。他面如死灰。
“到芬兰去了，”我重复一遍，诧异地望着他。“前天跑的。”

===Original===

“He got away﻿—ha! ha! ha!” Zorinsky dropped back into his seat. His momentary expression changed as suddenly as it had appeared, and he burst into uproarious laughter. “Do you *really* mean to say so? Ha! ha! My God, won’t they be wild! Damned clever! Don’t you know they’ve been turning the place upside down to find him? Ha, ha, ha! Now that really is good news, upon my soul!”
“Why should *you* be so glad about it?” I inquired. “You seemed at first to﻿—”
“I was astounded.” He spoke rapidly and a little excitedly. “Don’t you know Marsh was regarded as chief of allied organizations and a most dangerous man? But for some reason they were dead certain of catching him﻿—dead certain. Haven’t they got his wife, or his mother, or somebody, as hostage?”
“His wife.”
“It’ll go badly with her,” he laughed cruelly.
It was my turn to be startled. “What do you mean?” I said, striving to appear indifferent.
“They will shoot her.”
It was with difficulty that I maintained a tone of mere casual interest. “Do you really think they will shoot her?” I said, incredulously.
“Sure to,” he replied, emphatically. “What else do they take hostages for?”
For the rest of the evening I thought of nothing else but the possibility of Mrs. Marsh being shot. The Policeman had said the direct opposite, basing his statement on what he said was inside information. On the other hand, why on earth should hostages be taken if they were to be liberated when the culprits had fled? I could elicit nothing more from Zorinsky except that in his opinion Mrs. Marsh might be kept in prison a month or two, but in the long run would most undoubtedly be shot.
I listened but idly to the colonel, a pompous gentleman with a bushy white beard, who came in after dinner. Zorinsky told him he might speak freely in my presence and, sitting bolt upright, he conversed in a rather ponderous manner on the latest developments. He appeared to have a high opinion of Zorinsky. He confirmed the latter’s statements regarding radical changes in the organization of the army, and said Trotsky was planning to establish a similar new regime in the Baltic Fleet. I was not nearly so attentive as I ought to have been, and had to ask the colonel to repeat it all to me at our next meeting.

Maria was the only person I took into my confidence as to all my movements. Every morning I banged at the chalk-marked door.

===Chinese===

“跑了——哈！哈！哈！”佐林斯基跌回椅子里。他方才那一瞬的神色来得突然，去得也突然，随即爆发出一阵狂笑。“您说的*当真*？哈！哈！我的上帝，他们非气疯不可！太漂亮了！您不知道吧，他们为找他把这城里天翻地覆都翻遍了？哈，哈，哈！说真的，这可真是个好消息，千真万确！”
“您干嘛*这么*高兴？”我问。“您起初倒像是——”
“我是惊着了。”他话说得又快又有些激动。“您不知道马什被他们当作协约国组织的头子、最危险的人物？可不知为什么，他们先前拿准了能逮住他——拿得死死的。他们不是抓了他老婆、还是他母亲、反正是个人质吗？”
“他老婆。”
“她要吃苦头了，”他残忍地笑道。
这回轮到我吃惊了。“您这话什么意思？”我竭力做出无所谓的样子。
“会枪毙她的。”
我好不容易才维持住纯属随口闲谈的口气。“您真觉得他们会枪毙她？”我说，满脸不信。
“准毙，”他斩钉截铁。“不然抓人质干什么？”
那天晚上余下的时间，我满脑子只有马什太太可能被枪毙这一件事。警察说的恰恰相反，还说自己有内部消息。可话说回来，倘若人质注定要在正犯逃脱后获释，那当初又何苦抓人质？我再没能从佐林斯基嘴里套出别的，只除了他的看法：马什太太也许关上一两个月，归根到底必枪毙无疑。
饭后来的那位上校，一位白胡子浓密、派头十足的绅士，我没精打采地听着。佐林斯基告诉他在我面前可以直言无隐，他便直挺挺坐着，用相当迂缓的腔调纵论最新时局。他似乎对佐林斯基评价甚高。他证实了佐林斯基关于军队组织大变革的说法，又说托洛茨基正计划在波罗的海舰队建立类似的新规制。我心不在焉，远不如应该的那样专心，只好请上校在我们下次见面时把这一切再讲一遍。

玛丽亚（Maria）是我唯一推心置腹、将自己一切行动和盘托出的人。每天早晨，我去敲那扇画着粉笔记号的门。

===Original===

Maria let me in and I told her how things were going with Mrs. Marsh. Of course, I always gave her optimistic reports. Then I would say, “Tonight, Maria, I am staying at the Journalist’s﻿—you know his address﻿—tomorrow at Stepanovna’s, Friday night at Zorinsky’s, and Saturday, here. So if anything happens you will know where it probably occurred. If I disappear, wait a couple of days, and then get someone over the frontier﻿—perhaps the coachman will go﻿—and tell the British Consul.” Then I would give her my notes, written in minute handwriting on tracing paper, and she would hide them for me. Two more Englishmen left by Marsh’s route a few days after his departure and Maria gave them another small packet to carry, saying it was a letter from herself to Marsh. So it was, only on the same sheet as she had scrawled a pencil note to Marsh I wrote a long message in invisible ink. I made the ink by﻿—oh, it doesn’t matter how.
Zorinsky’s reports as to Melnikoff continued to be favourable. He hinted at a certain investigator who might have to be bought off, to which I gave eager assent. He gave me further information on political matters which proved to be quite accurate, and repellent though his bearing and appearance were, I began to feel less distrustful of him. It was about a week later, when I called him up, that he told me “the doctors had decided his brother was sufficiently well to leave hospital.” Tingling with excitement and expectation I hurried round.
“The investigator is our man,” explained Zorinsky, “and guarantees to let Melnikoff out within a month.”
“How will he do it?” I inquired.
“That rather depends. He may twist the evidence, but Melnikoff’s is a bad case and there’s not much evidence that isn’t damaging. If that’s too hard, he may swap Melnikoff’s dossier for somebody else’s and let the error be found out when it’s too late. But he’ll manage it all right.”
“And it must take a whole month?”
“Melnikoff will be freed about the middle of January. There’s no doubt about it. And the investigator wants 60,000 roubles.”
“*Sixty thousand roubles!*” I gasped. I was appalled at this unexpected figure. Where should I get the money from? The rouble was still worth about forty to the pound, so that this was some £1,500.
“Melnikoff’s case is a hopeless one,” said Zorinsky, dryly. “No one can let *him* off and go scot-free.

===Chinese===

玛丽亚放我进去，我把马什太太的近况讲给她听。当然，我报的总是乐观的消息。然后我总说：“今晚，玛丽亚，我宿在记者家——你知道他那儿——明晚在斯捷潘诺夫娜（Stepanovna）家，星期五在佐林斯基家，星期六在这儿。这样万一出事，你便知道多半出在哪里。我要是失踪了，等两天，然后设法让人过境——也许车夫肯去——告诉英国领事。”然后我把报告交给她，是用蝇头小字写在描图纸上的，她替我藏起来。马什走后几天，又有两个英国人循他的路线出境，玛丽亚托他们捎一小包东西，说是她自己写给马什的信。也确是她的信，只是在她用铅笔写给马什的那同一张纸上，我用密写墨水写了一封长信。墨水的制法是——哦，怎么制的无关紧要。
佐林斯基关于梅利尼科夫的消息继续向好。他暗示有位侦查员须要花钱打点，我热切应允。他进一步提供的政界消息后来证明相当准确，尽管他的做派和相貌叫人反感，我对他的戒心却渐渐松了。约莫一星期后，我给他挂电话，他告诉我，“医生们已断定他兄弟康复出院”了。我激动得浑身发麻，满怀期望赶了过去。
“侦查员是自己人了，”佐林斯基解释道，“保证不出一个月放梅利尼科夫出来。”
“他怎么个做法？”我问。
“那要看情形。他可以篡改证据，不过梅利尼科夫的案子糟糕，证据件件致命，能动的余地不大。若那条路太难走，他可以把梅利尼科夫的案卷跟别人的对调，等错处被发现，为时已晚。反正他会办妥的。”
“非要整整一个月不可吗？”
“一月中旬梅利尼科夫就能放出来。这没有疑问。侦查员要60,000卢布。”
“*六万卢布！*”我倒抽一口气。这突如其来的数目吓了我一跳。我上哪儿弄这笔钱去？卢布当时对英镑还值四十比一，这就合大约1,500英镑。
“梅利尼科夫的案子是死案，”佐林斯基干巴巴地说。“放了他还能不受牵连的人，一个也没有。

===Original===

The investigator wants to be guaranteed, for he will have to get over the frontier the same night, too. But I advise you to pay only half now, and the rest the day Melnikoff gets out. There will also be a few odd bribes to accomplices. Better allow 75,000 or 80,000 roubles all told.”
“I have very little money with me just now,” I said, “but I will try to get you the first 30,000 in two or three days.”
“And by the way,” he added, “I forgot to tell you last time you were here that I have seen Melnikoff’s sister, who is in the direst straits. Elena Ivanovna and I have sent her a little food, but she also needs money. We have no money, for we scarcely use it nowadays, but perhaps you could spare a thousand or so now and again.”
“I will give you some for her when I bring the other.”
“Thank you. She will be grateful. And now, unpleasant business over, let’s go and have a glass of vodka. Your health, Pavel Ivanitch.”
Rejoicing at the prospect of securing Melnikoff’s release, and burdened at the same time with the problem of procuring this large sum of money, I rang up next day the business friend of whom Marsh had spoken, using a prearranged password. Marsh called this gentleman the “Banker,” though that was not his profession, because he had left his finances in his charge. When I visited him I found him to be a man of agreeable though nervous deportment, very devoted to Marsh. He was unable to supply me with all the money I required, and I decided I must somehow get the rest from Finland, perhaps when I took Mrs. Marsh away.
The “Banker” had just returned from Moscow, whither he had been called with an invitation to accept a post in a new department created to check the ruin of industry. He was very sarcastic over the manner in which, he said, the “government of horny hands” (as the Bolsheviks frequently designate themselves) was beginning “to grovel before people who can read and write.” “In public speeches,” said the Banker, “they still have to call us ‘*bourzhu* (bourgeois) swine’ for the sake of appearances, but in private, when the doors are closed, it is very different. They have even ceased ‘comrading’: it is no longer ‘Comrade A.’ or ‘Comrade B.’ when they address us﻿—that honour they reserve for themselves﻿—but ‘Excuse me, Alexander Vladimirovitch,’ or ‘May I trouble you, Boris Konstantinovitch?’ ” He laughed ironically.

侦查员要的是保命的准头，因为他当夜也得跟着出境。不过我劝您现在只付一半，余下的等梅利尼科夫出来那天再付。另外还要打点几个同伙的小钱。统共您最好按75,000到80,000卢布预备。”
“我手头现钱很少，”我说，“不过我想法子在两三天里先给您凑30,000。”
“对了，”他又说，“上回忘了告诉您，我见了梅利尼科夫的妹妹，她穷到山穷水尽。叶莲娜·伊万诺夫娜和我送了她一点吃的，可她还需要钱。我们没有钱——如今我们几乎不用钱——不过您也许可以时不时匀个千把卢布。”
“我下次带钱来时一总给她。”
“谢谢。她会感激的。好了，正事完毕，咱们喝杯伏特加去。祝您健康，帕维尔·伊万内奇。”
一面为营救梅利尼科夫在望而欣喜，一面又为筹措这笔巨款发愁，第二天我给马什说起过的那位商界朋友挂了电话，用的是事先约定的暗语。马什管这位先生叫“银行家”，虽然那并非他的本行，只因马什把自己的财务托付给了他。见面之下，我发现他举止和蔼可亲而神经过敏，对马什一片忠心。他无法凑足我所需的全部款项，我决定其余部分无论如何得从芬兰弄来，也许带马什太太走的时候顺便解决。
“银行家”刚从莫斯科回来，他是应召去的，请他出山就任一个新设部门的职位，那部门是为挽救工业崩溃而设的。他说话极尽挖苦之能事，照他的说法，“长茧子的手的政府”（布尔什维克常这样自称）如今开始“向识字的人摇尾巴了”。“公开演说里，”银行家说，“为了装门面，他们还不得不骂我们‘bourzhu（资产阶级）猪猡’，可关起门来，就完全是另一回事了。他们连‘同志’都不‘同志’了：当面称呼我们，已不再是‘某同志’‘某同志’——那个光荣他们留给自己——而是‘对不起，亚历山大·弗拉基米罗维奇’‘麻烦您，鲍里斯·康斯坦丁诺维奇’了。”他讥诮地笑了。
